Robert Pattinson's James Bond Comments Resurface 14 Years Later Amid Casting Rumors

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Fresh off his electric performance in the dark, unsettling media satire "Primetime," Robert Pattinson is said to be in contention for the role of James Bond. According to The Hollywood Reporter, Pattinson screen tested for the part in Denis Villeneuve's upcoming Bond reboot movie for Amazon MGM Studios, which puts him in the running alongside several other well-known contenders. For the actor, it has been a long time coming. It seems that, 14 years ago, Pattinson envisioned stepping into the 007 role at a later point in his career.

In a 2012 interview with The Sun (via NDTV), the British star said he'd "definitely like to go for Bond" but wanted to wait another 20 years. "There'd be nothing worse than, like, 'Let's get a fresh-faced Bond!'" he added. "That would be the worst idea in the world. It would be ridiculous to reinvent it as some young posh kid." At the time, he was just starting to make his transition from teen heartthrob to chameleonic leading man, and was promoting what has since become known as one of Pattinson's best movies, David Cronenberg's "Cosmopolis." Since then, much has changed, and while it hasn't quite been two decades, it seems the actor wasn't kidding when he said he'd one day like to play 007.

Back in October 2026, it was reported that Josh O'Connor was one of the Bond finalists, alongside "Fantastic Beasts" actor Callum Turner, and "Slow Horses" star Jack Lowden. Since then, reports have claimed that Nicholas Hoult and Pattinson are also in the final group, which means five actors are now vying for the role of England's greatest spy. If Pattinson does land the role, it will be yet another major success for the actor and, given his prior comments, a particularly satisfying one.

In his interview with The Sun, Robert Pattinson seemed entirely opposed to the idea of a young James Bond. "After Daniel Craig you have to have some baggage," he said of the erstwhile Bond actor, who reinvented the spy for a new generation with the unbeatable "Casino Royale" in 2006. "I'd have to be tortured in the first few scenes. I'd have to do the first film with one arm or something." It's unclear just how serious Pattinson was at the time, but he seemed to have an awareness of just how tortured a version of Bond Craig portrayed during his tenure in the tux.

With that in mind, it will be interesting to see, if Pattinson does land the part, whether he doubles down on the haunted loner version of 007 he mentioned in his 2012 comments. The best Bonds have all had an edge to them, but it might be that Denis Villeneuve and Amazon MGM want to go in a different direction after the diminishing returns of the Craig era Bond films.

As it stands, nobody knows for sure who will play the lead role in Villeneuve's upcoming film. Based on the finalists, one thing we do know is that Amazon won't be going the "young posh kid" route, to use Pattinson's phrasing. All the contenders are 36 years old, except for Pattinson who at 40 is the oldest of the bunch. With final screen tests said to have already taken place, it won't be long before we get an official announcement, and if Pattinson does land the role we at least know he's been mentally preparing for 14 years.

Colin Farrell's Remake Of A '90s Sci-Fi Movie Is One Of Prime Video's Biggest Streaming Hits

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

"Total Recall" (2012) is directed by Len Wiseman, who most recently oversaw the 2025 "John Wick" offshoot "Ballerina" and previously helmed the "Underworld" films between 2003 and 2016. He is, unfortunately, also responsible for 2007's "Live Free or Die Hard," which, despite making the third spot on /Film's "Die Hard" movie ranking, we simply could have done without.

Unlike the 1990 original, "Total Recall" is set entirely on an Earth ravaged by chemical war, leaving only the United Federation of Britain (UFB) and the Colony (Australia) habitable. Whereas Arnold Schwarzenegger's Douglas Quaid had dreams of Mars, which turn out to be long-buried memories, Colin Farrell's counterpart finds himself experiencing nocturnal visions of being a secret agent. Bored of his factory job on The Colony, he enlists the company Rekall to implant artificial memories to match his dreams, which he soon learns are, in fact, memories of having worked as a UFB agent. Hunted by his former employers, he teams up with Jessica Biel's Melina, a member of a rebel group known as The Resistance. But as the pair try to outrun the UFB and their Chancellor Vilos Cohaagen (Bryan Cranston), the line between fantasy and reality becomes increasingly blurred.

Ahead of the film's debut, /Film asked: "Is 'Total Recall' the least essential film of the summer?" The answer, according to most critics, was yes. But it seems the Prime Video crowds are willing to give it another shot. Per streaming viewership tracker FlixPatrol, "Total Recall" is the No. 3 most-watched movie on Prime Video in the United States as of November 9, 2026. What's more, based on the film's trajectory thus far, it could be set for a chart-topping run. "Total Recall" debuted on the charts at No. 9 on November 7, 2026, before jumping to fourth the following day and hitting No. 3 the day after. By the end of the week, Farrell's forgotten remake might just hit No. 1 — not bad for a film with a 30% critic score on Rotten Tomatoes.

Both Philip K. Dick's story and the 1990 "Total Recall" film feature Mars. But the 2012 remake actually has more in common with Paul Verhoeven's rendition than the novel, using characters that only appear in the movie and adhering more closely to the big-screen storyline. That might have seemed like a reasonable approach, given how the first film was so successful. Unfortunately, Len Wiseman's effort didn't emulate the offbeat comedy of the original, which was so sharply rendered by Arnold Schwarzenegger and gave the film such a unique tone. Despite featuring some impressive action scenes, Wiseman's version also managed to feel like a generic sci-fi actioner, all of which combined to yield a less-than-impressive critical response.

That said, Roger Ebert was impressed enough to bestow three stars on the film — just half a star less than the 1990 version — and called it a "well-crafted, high-energy sci-fi." Still, even he had to admit that the film was one of several sci-fi movie remakes that should have never happened, ending his review by writing, "It never touched me emotionally, though, the way the 1990 film did, and strictly speaking, isn't necessary." Others were much more condemnatory, with David Sexton of the London Evening Standard giving the film one star and writing, "We used to do the future so much better. Let's not forget that. Let's forget this instead."

Normally, that should deter you from joining the "Total Recall" renaissance on Prime Video. But considering that, since the movie's debut, we've been subjected to an onslaught of streaming movie shabbiness, it might be worth a go to see if the remake really is bad or whether that's merely a memory implanted by the critics.

Destination Spotlight: Crystal Mountain

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Skiing at Crystal Mountain, Michigan. (Photo: Crystal Mountain)

As a proud Michigander, I spent many childhood summers “Up North,” a region of the state with beautiful lakes, cozy cabins, extensive hiking and biking trails, and every other manner of outdoor summer fun. But “Up North” isn’t strictly a summer destination; it’s a four-season adventure hub that offers everything from spectacular fall leaf-peeping to world-class winter skiing.

Among the best of these Michigan winter playgrounds is Crystal Mountain. Just a short drive from Traverse City and a stone’s throw from Sleeping Bear Dunes National Lakeshore, Crystal Mountain got its start 70 years ago with a single rope tow, an old car engine, and a dream. From these humble beginnings, it has blossomed into one of the best and most visited ski hills in the state, with 59 downhill runs, six chairlifts, two magic carpets, and more than 100 acres of skiable terrain.

The mission at the heart of the operation has always remained the same: to be a place where families come together to have fun, make memories, and experience all the best the outdoors have to offer. This philosophy has immortalized Crystal Mountain in the hearts of midwesterners like me, encouraging generation after generation of skiers to fall in love with the sport and the outdoors. It’s where I learned to ski—and where you can still take lessons from PSIA/AASI-trained instructors.

Beyond the ski hill, there’s no shortage of things to do at Crystal. Glide through this winter wonderland on cross-country skis—the resort’s 25 kilometers of trails were named best in the state by Midwest Living Magazine. Or let a team of Belgian horses do the gliding for you as you enjoy the views of the forests and hills from a horse-drawn carriage. Lace up your ice skates in the heart of the resort’s village at Barr Park, or explore the Michigan Legacy Art Park, a 30-acre reserve located on the grounds of Crystal Mountain where you can snowshoe, hike, and take in the sculptures and scenery.

When you’ve had enough of the cold, unwind at the outdoor hot tub, the indoor pool at the Peak Fitness Center, or the award-winning Crystal Spa. Consistently recognized as one of the best in the country, Crystal Spa features infrared saunas, eucalyptus steam rooms, and a relaxation lounge with a cozy fireplace, making it the perfect oasis after a day spent out in the winter weather.

There are endless ways to enjoy Crystal Mountain, but the true magic of this place can’t be credited to any one thing—it’s the feeling you get when you’re here. It’s the memories you’ll cherish for a lifetime.

Established in 1956, Crystal Mountain is a family-owned, four-season resort located 28 miles southwest of Traverse City, Michigan, and a short drive from Sleeping Bear Dunes National Lakeshore. The 1,500-acre resort features lodging, dining, downhill and cross-country skiing, two championship golf courses, shopping, the award-winning Crystal Spa, Michigan’s only alpine slide, Peak Fitness, the Park at Water’s Edge, Michigan Legacy Art Park, weddings, a conference center, and real estate options.

Roger Ebert Called Disney's Attempt To Update A Classic Cartoon Character 'Transcendently Bad'

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Kids of today likely won't have heard of the visually-impaired cartoon millionaire known as Mr. Magoo. But for a large part of the 20th Century he was a pop culture fixture who entertained generations of kids with his slapstick shenanigans. By 1997, however, his appeal had waned, which makes Disney's decision to reboot the IP with a Leslie Nielsen-led live-action film all the more confounding. Thankfully, Roger Ebert was on-hand to let the studio have it — which he dutifully did with a scathing review that, bizarrely enough, actually awarded the film half a star.

The critic handed out some damning reviews in his time. Ebert hated Leonard Nimoy's box office flop "Funny About Love" and even walked out of the Oscar-winning war movie "Mediterraneo." All of which left filmmakers terrified of being handed the dreaded "thumbs down" for their efforts. Around 60 movies earned this mark of shame during the critic's career but somehow, "Mr. Magoo" managed to eke out half a star. If you read Ebert's thoughts on the film you'll no doubt wonder how it managed such a feat.

In his review, Ebert revealed that the only thing he liked about the film was the titular character's red Studebaker — that was it. Otherwise, he considered "Mr. Magoo" to be so bad it "soars above ordinary badness as the eagle outreaches the fly." How, then, did this ill-advised comedy reboot manage to get a half-star out of the old boy? Did that Studebaker really save "Mr. Magoo" from the thumbs down club? It's hard to imagine given just how much Ebert hated this movie.

There are plenty of movies Roger Ebert hated that are worth watching, but "Mr. Magoo" isn't one of them. The character first appeared in the 1949 theatrical short "The Ragtime Bear," where he was voiced by none other than future "Gilligan's Island" star and legendary character actor Jim Backus. Backus voiced the United Productions of America (UPA) character until 1977, portraying him in theatrical cartoons, TV series, and TV specials. While Mr. Magoo's character developed over the course of his existence, he's best known as a lovably oblivious old millionaire, whose extreme nearsightedness sees him wander into dangerous situations only to miraculously escape unharmed — causing significant distress to his nephew and frequent sidekick Waldo.

While Backus stopped voicing the character after the 1977 TV series "What's New, Mr. Magoo?" the visually-impaired millionaire endured. The most recent incarnation of the character appeared in the "Mr. Magoo" cartoon, which wrapped up in 2023. Prior to that, however, Disney decided that kids of the mid-'90s needed to be reintroduced to Quincy Magoo, and hired Hong Kong filmmaker Stanley Tong to make it happen.

In what turned out to be Tong's one and only English-language film, the frequent Jackie Chan collaborator teamed up not with a martial arts legend but with Leslie Nielsen. The "Naked Gun" star played the eponymous millionaire in a film that saw him hunted by the FBI and criminals eager to recover a priceless gem to which Mr. Magoo is entirely oblivious. Much of the movie follows the tried-and-true formula of its title character getting himself into absurd situations and coming out unscathed, such as when he commandeers an ironing board to ski after thieves. The movie made $28.9 million on a $30 million budget and upset Roger Ebert considerably.

In his half-star review of "Mr. Magoo," Roger Ebert described the film as being "transcendently bad," and accused it of being entirely without laughs. The critic admitted to never having been a fan of the original cartoons, which he recalled as essentially telling the same joke over and over. Likewise, the film was, in Ebert's estimation, "a one-joke movie without the joke."

Interestingly enough, this might be the only time he ever took aim at the bloopers, which play during the film's credits and evidently failed to amuse the critic. "Even the outtakes at the end aren't funny," he wrote, "and I'm not sure I understood one of them, unless it was meant to show stunt people hilariously almost being drowned." Ebert wasn't even impressed with Leslie Nielsen, who'd long since established himself as a comedic force but was, according to the reviewer, "unable to overcome the fact that a little Magoo at six minutes in a cartoon is a far different matter than a lot of Magoo at 90 minutes in a feature."

On "Siskel & Ebert," neither of the hosts could bring themselves to say anything nice about the movie. Gene Siskel called it "uninspired" before Ebert took the opportunity to once again lay into the movie, calling it a "wet blanket." Roger Ebert was wrong about a fair few movies in his time, but a 10% critic score on Rotten Tomatoes suggests he was right on the money with this one. And yet, he gave the movie half a star. Why? Did the Studebaker really save it from the shame of a thumbs down rating?

Iran war helps fuel global drop in fossil fuel emissions — but there’s a catch

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In the run-up to the COP31 U.N. climate change conference, scientists have revealed that global carbon emissions from fossil fuels are projected to drop by 0.4% this year, driven by growth in installed wind and solar power capacity, as well as oil price hikes caused by the Iran conflict. But climate goals are still unlikely to be met.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The world's carbon emissions from oil, gas and coal are projected to decrease by 0.4% this year, driven partly by the expansion of solar and wind power capacity and the price increases caused by the Iran conflict, according to a new report.

The 2026 Global Carbon Budget report was published in the journal Earth System Science Data Nov. 10 and is due to be presented at the U.N. Climate Change Conference's COP31 meeting in Antalya, Turkey, this week.

Global carbon emissions have increased steadily since record-keeping began. This year is projected to have a slight decrease in fossil-fuel emissions, but that is partly due to price increases caused by the Iran conflict.

It is the first time annual carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions from fossil fuels have fallen since the coronavirus pandemic in 2020. But the overall emissions picture suggests the world is still likely to miss key climate goals.

"It is an encouraging sign that the growth of emissions is slowing and decarbonisation efforts are starting to make a difference," said Joeri Rogelj, a climate scientist at the Centre for Environmental Policy at Imperial College London who wasn't involved in the research. "However, at 0.4%, the projected decline is very small," Rogelj told Live Science in an email.

The report projects 38.2 billion metric tons (42.1 billion U.S. tons) of emissions of the climate-warming greenhouse gas CO2 from fossil fuel sources this year. Greenhouse gases trap heat by absorbing the sun's radiation, and as their concentrations in the atmosphere increase, so do global average temperatures, leading to altered weather patterns and higher sea levels, as well as other major impacts.

The war with Iran has resulted in restricted fuel availability and high prices, with a corresponding reduction in demand

Part of the 0.4% reduction in CO2 emissions is attributable to 40 countries that have cut emissions over the past decade while growing their economies. These include the U.S., Australia, New Zealand, South Korea, the United Kingdom, Japan and about 30 countries in the European Union, the researchers reported.

When it comes to some of the biggest emitters, annual CO2 emissions are projected to decrease by 0.9% in China, 1.3% in the U.S., 1.5% in the European Union and 1% in Japan, but India's annual CO2 emissions are projected to grow by 4.4%.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

Carbon emissions are projected to decrease slightly in the U.S., China and the E.U. but to increase in India.

Fossil-fuel-linked CO2 emissions from the rest of the world are projected to decrease by 0.7%, driven mainly by a 1.9% drop in oil emissions. The report authors suggested that this is a short-term response to the global supply constraints and increased prices triggered by the Iran conflict.

"The war with Iran has resulted in restricted fuel availability and high prices, with a corresponding reduction in demand," Rogelj said.

While fossil fuel emissions and demand for oil are declining globally, overall demand for energy is actually increasing.

"Based only on the demand for energy, which has grown, we would have expected an increase in fossil CO2 emissions by about 0.8 gigatonnes," said study co-author Judith Hauck, a biogeoscientist at the Alfred Wegener Institute, Helmholtz Centre for Polar and Marine Research in Germany.

About two-thirds of that increase in demand is being met by solar and wind power and electric vehicles, showing that clean technologies are making a real difference in reducing global emissions, Hauck said. However, the hike in energy prices that resulted from the Iran conflict also decreased demand by 0.4%, she said.

"So only together with the energy crisis did we bring the change in emissions down to negative," Hauck told Live Science, adding that she expects CO2 emissions to rebound if the conflict ends, as they did after the temporary emissions drop caused by the lockdowns during the COVID-19 pandemic.

The exceptional circumstances that energy markets experienced this year make it clear that the small projected emissions decline is no reason to declare that emissions are now reducing, but there are hopeful signs, Rogelj said.

"Despite my muted enthusiasm about whether these lower emissions for 2026 represent the start of a long-term decline, the analysis shows encouraging signs of deeper long-term structural changes," he said. "But this shift must accelerate to ensure it is not outpaced by energy demand growth."

The report found that clean energy growth isn't yet fast enough to drive a steady decline in global CO2 emissions attributable to fossil fuels. The study authors expect that to happen in the next few years, Hauck said, as long as there is a continued push to deploy solar and wind power, batteries and electric vehicles.

CO2 emissions from fossil fuels are just part of the picture, though. Because of increases in human-caused peat fires, deforestation and forest fires linked to dry El Niño conditions, total global CO2 emissions from all sources this year are projected to increase slightly from 42.8 billion metric tons (47.2 billion U.S. tons) last year to reach a record 43.3 billion metric tons (47.7 billion U.S. tons) this year, according to the report.

This means that the total budget of CO2 that can be emitted before global warming reaches 1.5 degrees Celsius (2.7 degrees Fahrenheit) above preindustrial levels is now just 150 billion metric tons (165 billion U.S. tons) ‪—‬ equivalent to less than four years of emissions at the current rate.

Hauck, like many climate scientists, thinks we have already missed our chance to hit the 1.5 C target and could only get back to that level through extensive removal of CO2 from the atmosphere.

"I'm still hopeful for something between 1.5 and 2 C [3.6 F]," Hauck said. "But for 1.5 degrees, we would need to see such a drastic strong drop in emissions. Based on what we've seen in the past years, even with this strong increase in avoided emissions, I don't think we can make that."

To halt global warming from increasing ever further, the world needs to be more ambitious in its planned emissions cuts, said Rogeli. We need to bring annual emissions down to zero to protect future generations from a double whammy of “having to try to transform economies so that they produce less climate pollution, while dealing with the impacts and damages of an increasingly adverse climate system," he said.

Chris Simms is a freelance journalist who previously worked at New Scientist for more than 10 years, in roles including chief subeditor and assistant news editor. He was also a senior subeditor at Nature and has a degree in zoology from Queen Mary University of London. In recent years, he has written numerous articles for New Scientist and in 2018 was shortlisted for Best Newcomer at the Association of British Science Writers awards.

Humble Hauler autonomous trailer takes the truck out of trucking [update]

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Less than a year after its founding, truckless trucking brand Humble Robotics has secured more than $75 million after a $51 million Series A funding round that will fuel the company’s transition from prototype to preproduction.

The latest funding round for the driverless, cab-less, and truck-less battery-electric freight platform developer was led by Obvious Ventures with participation from other existing investors Eclipse and Energy Impact Partners. Humble said it will use the funding for customer pilots, testing and validation, and early manufacturing.

“We’ve spent years proving that a truck can drive itself,” explains Humble Robotics founder and CEO, Eyal Cohen. “The harder question is how to make autonomy something a freight operator can depend on every day.”

We originally covered the Humble Hauler back in May. You can check out the original article, below, then let us know what you think of the company’s prospects in the comments.

The startup that dares to ask, “Why does the Macbook driving my freight around need a whole cab?” just showed off a fully driverless, cab-less, and truck-less battery-electric freight platform designed to handle containerized cargo more efficiently than ever.

Instead of looking at the tractor and asking how to make it work without a driver, Humble looked at the trailer, itself, redesigning the container chassis as a vehicle from the ground up. The end result isn’t just an autonomous solution, it’s an overall lighter platform without a cab that saves shippers the weight of a sleeper cab, a day cab, or the truck at all.

The Humble approach is said to improve payload efficiency of shipping containers, and intended for dock-to-dock operation.

“I have dedicated my career to building electric and autonomous vehicle technology,” explains Eyal Cohen, Humble Robotics’ founder and CEO. “For the first time, freight can be fully automated all the way to the loading dock. We are making freight sustainable, safe and efficient in a way no one thought was possible. And we’re doing it with an exceptional team of industry veterans and AV experts.”

Emerging from stealth after a $24 million funding round, Humble claims to have completed its first Humble Hauler prototype in less than six months – and they’ll soon be ready to begin autonomous testing in pilot programs with a number of logistics partners.

The vehicle uses a combination of cameras, lidar, and radar for 360-degree awareness (unhindered by seeing around a cab), and uses vision-language-action models to help the system respond to new scenarios.

Thanks to superior battery tech, a maturing global charging infrastructure, and a permanently damaged MidEast oil supply, there’s never been a better time to be an EV startup. Humble is hitting all the electric trailer buzzwords, too, while also touting the fact that the electric powertrain can reduce a company’s to volatile fuel prices even as it helps produce other cost benefits like lower maintenance costs.

There is a reason why the transportation industry developed separate cabs and trailers. Trailers can be loaded or unloaded for days and are much less expensive than trucks that serve both purposes in one piece. That allows the more expensive piece of the transport puzzle to be used more and allows the trailer to be loaded and unloaded while the cab is elsewhere working or in the shop. Query whether the savings they cite will surpass the costs of not having the flexibility of a cab and trailer design.

And if a robot like this can deliver on its promises without the baked-in inefficiencies of humanoids, they might be one step closer to provide that the most efficient solution to a problem is rarely a general one, and even rarer is it a solution that looks like a human.

Twitch and Amazon want to turn “git gud” into “u good?” for men’s mental health

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Online multiplayer gamers face genuine psychological warfare. Don’t believe me? Log on to your local Apex Legends, Call of Duty, or League of Legends lobby and let the “git guds” (and, well, lots of other stuff we’d rather not print) roll in.

But Twitch, its parent Amazon, and a new organization called GamerCounsel are hoping to change “git gud” into “u gud?”

Timed for Movember, an annual monthlong recognition of the need for more thorough and compassionate healthcare for men, the Squad Support campaign taps Australian Twitch streamers Bajo, Crayator, Jack Buzza, and Midbeast to promote checking in our your gamer buds and–when needed–chatting with licensed professionals about what’s on your mind.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

The licensed experts Squad Support recommends are, of course, those from GamerCounsel. The service launched in 2026, and says it brings “professional counselling and psychotherapy into gaming and interactive online spaces.”

Just like an IRL mental health office, GamerCounsel intakes clients and matches them with qualified therapists. The twist here is that therapists aren’t sitting behind desks and clients aren’t lying on couches.

Instead, they’re both squadding up in the same video game.

GamerCounsel therapists and clients (in both one-on-one and group settings) meet remotely over platforms like Discord and work through an appointment while button-mashing together. The org’s website recommends games like Minecraft, Roblox, Fortnite, Rocket League, Stardew Valley, Among Us, Animal Crossing, Mario Kart, Fall Guys, and Destiny, but says its services extend to the thousands of other games on Steam, too–so clients don’t seem super limited in what they can pick.

According to ContentGrip, GamerCounsel is allotting 500 hours of support time to people who see the Squad Support campaign and want to access its gaming-themed help.

A graphic for the campaign illustrates the constant mixed messages male gamers might want to work through with a therapist: “Git gud. But ask for help. Be a man. But don’t be masculine. Share your feelings. But don’t give the ick. Make the first move. But don’t be a creep. Be romantic. But don’t simp. Be the provider. But be present. Be strong. Be vulnerable. Toughen up. Open up. Green flag. Red flag. Red pill. Blue pill. Say less. Say more.”

The Squad Support campaign is going out through Amazon Ads, and includes Twitch components like a new “U GUD?” emote, interactive polls, fundraising tools, and use of Twitch’s Stream Together feature, which Bajo, Crayator, Jack Buzza, Midbeast, and other creators can use to host Movember experts on their streams.

“Gen Z and young millennials are connecting through gaming, and Squad Support meets them where they are. Our first collaboration with Movember brings together a shared ambition to reach young people through the communities and experiences they already value,” Willie Pang, Managing Director of Amazon Ads Australia and New Zealand, said in a statement. “By combining Twitch creators, engaged communities and interactive experiences, Amazon Ads, through Twitch’s Brand Partnership Studio, is demonstrating how brands can go beyond traditional advertising to show up in culture and build meaningful connections with audiences that are notoriously difficult to reach and engage.”

Folks who want to access Squad Support’s free help from GamerCounsel must be 18+ and located in Australia. The org asks that people submit applications, and it will accommodate as many as possible into solo and group therapy sessions.

Here at Tubefilter, we’ve talked many times about creators’ need for mental health support. But this need extends beyond individual communities. Everyone needs more mental health support–especially people who spend their lives being told they shouldn’t.

How the ‘blue tsunami’ crested in North Carolina

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

LaKesha Womack faced towering odds as an underfunded Democrat making her first run for public office in a North Carolina district that Donald Trump carried by 15 points two years ago.

But as early voting surged in mid-October, she sensed she might pull off an upset over Republican Rep. Tim Moore, the influential former speaker of the North Carolina House and a chief architect of the gerrymander that aimed to keep the 14th District safely red.

“There were a lot of pins and needles,” said Womack, a 49-year-old ordained minister who works at a Charlotte-based nonprofit. After seeing long lines at polling places extend into Election Day, she grew more confident: “People were ready to exercise their voice.”

Womack – who raised less than $60,000 to Moore’s $2.1 million – eked out a 2-point win in the district, which reaches from the Charlotte suburbs to mountain counties in the west.

Saddled with an unpopular president and broad dissatisfaction with the status quo, Republican incumbents across the nation experienced a midterm drubbing last week. As of Monday evening, House Democrats had netted 42 seats, with more races left to be called. The GOP in North Carolina, a state that Trump carried three times, was among the hardest hit.

Democrats have so far flipped five House seats in the Tar Heel State. Besides Womack, the “blue tsunami” also swept in Paul Barringer, a lawyer who founded an outdoor clothing line, and High Point Mayor Cyril Jefferson. And Army veteran Richard Ojeda, who once served in the West Virginia Senate, defeated Rep. Richard Hudson, the chairman of the National Republican Congressional Committee.

Democrats also won two races that had been seen as competitive for much of the cycle. Farmer Jamie Ager flipped an open seat in the state’s mountain west, while Rep. Don Davis held on to his Eastern North Carolina district, which Republicans had redrawn in their favor.

Meanwhile, in the state’s hilly northwest corner, 11-term Republican Virginia Foxx, the chairwoman of the powerful Rules Committee, is fighting for her political future: As of Monday afternoon, former journalist Chuck Hubbard led her by 99 votes in the 5th District, though The Associated Press had yet to call the race.

That was also true in the neighboring 10th District, where GOP incumbent Pat Harrigan had a 1-point edge over Democratic physician associate Ashley Bell. Also uncalled were a pair of contests in Eastern North Carolina, where Republican Rep. Greg Murphy clung to a 233-vote edge over Democratic former state Rep. Raymond Smith and GOP Rep. David Rouzer led Democratic social worker Kimberly Hardy by less than a point.

“Nobody could have predicted this,’’ said David Wilson Brown, who leads the Democratic Party in Gaston County near Charlotte and has lost two bids for the House, including to Foxx in 2020. “It’s a watershed moment.”

The Democratic rout in the midterm elections was powered by victories in states with new GOP-favored maps, including Texas, Ohio and Florida.

But Democrats’ success in North Carolina was especially striking given the steep structural disadvantages the party faced. Since Republicans flipped the state legislature in 2010, and with it control of the redistricting process, no House GOP incumbent had lost a general election in the Tar Heel State until last week.

This year’s  congressional map – the state’s sixth in seven election cycles – was engineered by the Republican-led legislature to help the party capture 11 of the state’s 14 House seats.

But the Democratic surge was so big that it easily overpowered the GOP’s built-in edge. Democratic candidates were also lifted by former Gov. Roy Cooper, whose 20-point Senate win provided the underticket with powerful coattails.

“In order for them to gerrymander our state in the way that they did, they were spread very thin,” Brown said, referring to state Republicans. “So they were kind of ripe to be defeated that way.”

The repeated rounds of redistricting may have also erased some of the advantages of incumbency, according to Peter L. Francia, a political science professor at East Carolina University. “If you carve up a district every two years, you’ve taken away some of that [advantage]. And I think that that came back and hurt Republicans in this cycle.”

Doug Heye, a GOP strategist and a former spokesman for the Republican National Committee, said redistricting “ticked off a whole lot of voters, and really activated the Democratic base.”

On the Republican side, voters were “less motivated because they were unexcited about Trump,’’ Heye added.

Republican incumbents accustomed to winning by comfortable margins weren’t prepared for a political environment driven by anger over the war in Iran and angst about the rising cost of living.

“Clearly, a lot of those Republicans didn’t take their elections seriously,” Heye said. “They won last time with 18 points. They weren’t on anyone’s radar screen, so they didn’t think they had to do anything.”

Womack credits her win to a grassroots campaign centered on economic issues. While her race wasn’t on the national radar – Moore, after all, won by 16 points in 2024 – and her campaign didn’t have money for ads, she was out in the district doing events every day.

“There are a lot of working families right now who are in the missing middle,” she said in an interview. “They make too much money to get help, but they don’t make enough money to get ahead. … Someone who can be working a full-time job, but still doesn’t have enough money to buy groceries or to afford their medications or to have access to health insurance.”

Womack said her own struggles have shaped her approach to politics. This week, she’s slated to appear in court on a felony charge for allegedly failing to return a rental car in 2019. The incident wound up in one of Moore’s attack ads.

“There are so many people who [understand] what happens when you’re down on your luck and you don’t have enough money to make good financial decisions,’’ she said. “Most people don’t want those bad decisions to be a definition of their character for the rest of their life.”

Early in the midterm cycle, Democratic opposition research group American Bridge prepared detailed dossiers on Republican House incumbents in North Carolina.

“Someone like Virginia Foxx, she’s been on the Hill forever,” said Pat Dennis, the group’s president. “She was somebody … who under the radar could be vulnerable. So we started really early.”

American Bridge’s political arm spent $340,000 on ads targeting Foxx. One spot featured a man who identified himself as a Trump voter expressing anger at the government’s response to Hurricane Helene, which devastated the mountain communities of Western North Carolina two years ago.

Other Democrats scoffed at the group’s decision to spend money targeting entrenched Republicans far from the main House battleground.

“There was a lot of reluctance from people to really believe that that data was correct, but it was,’’ Dennis said. “Then over the summer, we saw her numbers collapsing, so we put paid media behind it and it really paid off.”

Hubbard ran against Foxx two years ago and lost by almost 20 points.

“I kind of had a notion I was going to do this again,’’ he said in an interview Saturday while en route to Washington for freshman orientation. “I don’t like to lose at checkers, much less a congressional race.”

After a court-drawn map resulted in a split North Carolina delegation after the 2022 elections, state Republicans won back control of the map-making process and went on to win 10 of the state’s 14 House seats under a new map two years later.

At Trump’s urging, Republicans made further tweaks to the North Carolina map last year, but the original gerrymander finally came back to bite the GOP in 2026, according to Hubbard.

“North Carolina is one of the most severely gerrymandered states,’’ he said. “And what happened was they had to steal from Peter to pay Paul. It’s been referred to by political scientists as a dummymander.”

As the vote count in the 5th District continues, Foxx, whose campaign declined to comment for this story, has requested the presence of nonpartisan election observers.

They “will serve as important, watchful eyes to ensure that this sacred process is not impeded and that all legal votes are counted in a consistent, thorough manner,’’ she posted on social media.

But even before the final races are called, Republicans are already looking to the next election.

Heye, who expects North Carolina to remain a purple state in 2028 and beyond, cautioned the Democrats who won last week not to get too comfortable in Washington. He invoked Fred Heineman, the former Raleigh police chief who rode the 1994 “Republican Revolution” to Congress – and lasted a single term.

“Some of them will lose, and some of the people who lost may run two years from now and get their seat back,’’ he said.

One GOP operative, who requested anonymity to speak candidly, offered up a similar assessment.

Trump “didn’t keep his promises” and that “just got voters very angry and suppressed the GOP base from coming out because, in a midterm election it’s a base turnout contest,’’ the strategist said.

“But these Democrats are renting these districts for two years.”

Judge Rejects Motion to Dismiss Lindsay Clancy’s Murder Charges

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A Massachusetts judge denied a bid from Lindsay Clancy‘s lawyers to have the murder charges against her dismissed, clearing the way for a second trial after the first ended in a mistrial after a jury failed to reach a consensus verdict.

“The court understands the frustration of the defendant and the jurors that despite the utmost diligence, the jury ultimately was unable to reach a verdict in this emotionally grueling and complicated case,” Judge William Sullivan said on Monday in an order, per The New York Times.

Sullivan rejected the defense attorneys’ argument that a retrial would violate Clancy’s protection under the double jeopardy clause of the U.S. Constitution’s Fifth Amendment, a legal rule that protects an accused person against being prosecuted twice for the same crime. They claimed the judge acted prematurely in declaring a mistrial and should have questioned the holdout juror who voted to convict Clancy.

Sullivan wrote on Monday that it would have been “impermissibly coercive” to question the sole holdout juror, Michael P. Desronvil, during the trial, and that the other jurors had indicated “a disagreement about the substance of deliberations,” not an issue with Desronvil’s ability to deliberate.”

The judge also rejected a bid from Clancy’s lawyer, Kevin Reddington, to investigate the holdout. The attorney had requested permission to access Desronvil’s cellphone records, search data, and the confidential questionnaire that he had answered during jury selection. Sullivan said that the probe “would be an improper intrusion into this juror’s privacy and would serve to discourage citizens from being willing to serve as jurors in the future.”

In a small win for the defense, Sullivan issued a separate ruling rejecting the prosecution’s request for a gag order that would bar Clancy’s lawyers from speaking with the media about the case. Trending Stories Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’ Pink Becomes First Solo Female Pop Artist to Headline the Sphere Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr to Reunite at Las Vegas Show in 2027 Trump Finally Gets Around to Claiming the Midterms Were Rigged

Last month, Sullivan dismissed a request from Clancy‘s legal team to find the mother not guilty of killing her three children after her high-profile case ended in a mistrial. Sullivan denied the lawyers’ arguments that prosecutors failed to present enough evidence during the trial that Clancy murdered her three children — Cora, five; Dawson, three; and Callan, eight months — in January 2023.

Both parties are expected to meet early next week to discuss the next steps in the case.

How Useful Is An Alpine Climbing Award, Really?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Ultar Sar team, of Sebastian Pelletti, Ethan Berman, and Maarten Van Haeren, accept their Piolet d'Or in Saint-Christophe-En-Oisans, France, in October 2026. (Photo: Piotr Drożdż)

When I learned I’d be attending a gala for the “Oscars of Alpinism,” I made a note to pack formal clothes. The Piolets d’Or, or Golden Ice Axes, is a big deal; a rare career-building opportunity in a professional alpinist’s life. Earning this award can transform a strong amateur into a full-fledged member of global alpinism’s elite. This year, I joined the recipients who’d been coaxed out of their humble mountain hideouts to partake in alpinism’s biggest award ceremony.

Established in 1992, the Piolets have a respectable mission: to promote difficult, alpine-style new routes on the world’s great mountains. But the award has weathered plenty of criticism over the years. After rejecting his Piolet nomination in 2006, U.K. legend Ian Parnell later wrote in Alpinist: “Is it possible to choose a winner in an inherently unquantifiable pursuit [like alpinism]?” Others decry the Piolets d’Or for awarding climbers who recently died in the mountains, as well as for holding a subjective line of what counts as good climbing style.

I’ve been critical of the Piolets at times myself, especially after hearing one well-known alpinist talk longingly of wanting to climb something worthy of the award. I winced thinking about anyone tackling runout mixed terrain at high altitude with a golden ice axe taunting them overhead.

I thought of these critics, and my own biases, during my transatlantic flight a few weeks ago to this year’s ceremony in Saint-Christophe-en-Oisans, France. The award clearly has a dark side. But it has also become increasingly important in the year 2026. Lately, I’ve watched the professional climbing landscape bend to the pressures of sponsorship and social-media engagement—physical performance is but one metric to make a modern-day alpinist’s career.

In this new landscape, we see some climbers eager to make a quick buck. Others orient ascents around appeasing future sponsors or gaining attention online. In some instances, this materializes as full-scale fraud. In others, imaginative white lies of what constitutes a “summit.”

Through this lens, the Piolets d’Or represents an institution that is slowly becoming necessary. Nearly every year for the last 34 years, a varied, rotating cast of legendary alpinists sifts through the reams of new climbing achievements. Tossing aside marketing fluff and half-baked route attempts, they present to the world the year’s genuine highpoints of alpinism.

I wanted to know: Does the good of the Piolets d’Or outweigh the bad?

The singular Piolet d’Or first launched with the aim of recognizing the greatest alpine ascent of the previous year. In true Oscars’ fashion, at the early Piolet, all of the potential candidates were invited to an in-person ceremony, where the sole winner was read out, for the first time, in front of a couple thousand people.

“It was a mess,” British alpinist Paul Ramsden, one of this year’s jury members, told me of the Piolet’s early days. He and countryman Mick Fowler had been invited to the ceremony in Paris in 2003, when their ascent of Siguniang (6,250m) in China was in the running. “We all got up onto the stage, and they read my and Mick’s names out, and a French team beside us burst into tears,” Ramsden said.

American Dean Potter was on stage too, in the running for his solo ascents of the Patagonian spires Cerro Torre and Fitz Roy. When he got snubbed, he was livid, Ramsden remembered. Potter screamed at the organizers, asking why they’d told him to fly all the way to Europe to stand on stage for an award they knew he’d never receive.

Later that evening, in a sign of the early Piolet d’Or attitude, a special party took place offsite to celebrate Ramsden and Fowler’s win. With his golden ice axe in hand, Ramsden remembered ambling over to the afterparty, only to be stopped by a bouncer. The club had a dress code, and Ramsden was wearing sneakers. “So we weren’t allowed into our own party,” Ramsden laughed.

The Piolet d’Or ran uninterrupted from 1992 to 2007, when mounting criticism came to a head. Most notable was from the Slovenian alpinist Marko Prezelj, who was invited to that year’s event after his ascent of Chomo Lhari (7,326m), on the border of Tibet. He walked slowly onto the stage, took a long pause, then refused to accept his award, explaining why he thought the Piolet was antithetical to his view of alpinism. “Comparing different climbs is not possible without some kind of personal involvement and even then it is difficult,” Prezelj later wrote. “Last year I climbed in Alaska, Patagonia, and Tibet. I cannot decide which expedition was the most… the ‘most what’ in fact?”

Prezelj and others had become increasingly uneasy with how the Piolet d’Or pitted alpinists against each other. With only one winner, everyone else, in their eyes, became a loser. Such was not the spirit of alpinism.

The organizers cancelled the ceremony in 2008 and took a beat to reimagine it. A year later, after personnel changes and plenty of disagreements, a new version of the Piolet emerged, complete with an updated charter. The now-plural Piolets rewarded a climb’s lightweight style, self-sufficiency, technical difficulty, and management of objective hazards. Most importantly, though, the Piolets adopted a more community-based attitude. Everyone who was nominated for the award was given a Piolet d’Or.

This year’s ceremony was devoid of the drama that tainted the Piolet’s past. No one walked onto the stage just to reject the award. There was no French team in tears, and—as far as I could tell—no leery feeling of one-upsmanship among the award recipients. It was a bunch of climbers, wearing sneakers and duct-taped down jackets, drinking a bit too much red wine.

Paul Ramsden, who is 57, has been to a number of these week-long parties over the years, five times as a recipient—the most of any climber—and multiple times again as a jury member. Despite the criticism it’s received over the years, and his own jarring experience in 2003, he is now a proud supporter of the Piolets d’Or.

“I see awful style from climbers in some parts of the world,” he told me, citing large teams with bolt kits and hundreds of meters of fixed ropes, all but assured to succeed with enough dogged, industrial determination. “By rewarding alpine-style climbs, we’re not really rewarding the recipients. We’re signalling to the rest of the climbing community what ‘good’ style is.” In practice, that means that if a climber doesn’t have the skill to scale an unclimbed face with the same hardware they might take to a crag, they should leave the objective for a future generation of climbers who can.

One alpinist, who wished to remain anonymous, but has been nominated for a Piolet before, emphasized how flimsy and subjective any notion of “good” climbing style is. In a phone call with me, they noted how the Piolets jury considers climbing in insulated boots and ultralight crampons with Google-Earth reconnaissance as “good” climbing style, but considered carrying a few extra ropes to fix sections of the route poor form. The jury considers climbing in a lightweight team of two an enviable expression of alpine self sufficiency, yet a team of five or six is antiquated and ungainly.

Even understated, minimalist climbing expeditions to the Himalaya these days require a cottage-industry of resources: international flights, bus rides, porters, cook tents, tens of thousands of dollars of equipment—the list goes on. Deciding what the “correct” amount of resources an expedition should use is an inexact science, and one which the Piolets jury is still figuring out.

“I think it’s cool that we have a pretty objective governing body deciding what is actually good for alpine climbing,” Australian-Argentine climber Sebastian Pelletti, a recipient of a Piolet this year, told me. Like me, Pelletti has noticed professional alpinists erroneously claiming a “new” route to their sponsors and the world.

Last year, I wrote about one of those so-called new routes on Jirishanca (6,094m), in Peru’s Cordillera Huayhuash. Three very famous European alpinists climbed about a thousand meters of new terrain to the mountain’s summit ridge. Above, a spine of unconsolidated snow—the crux of the mountain—proved too difficult for the team. They bailed. A few days later, the climbers and their sponsors plastered a topo for this new route far and wide, drawing a bright red line that stopped on Jirishanca’s “east summit.” The east summit does not exist, nor has it ever been considered a final destination on the mountain. It is a brief bump in an otherwise vertical landscape, at least six pitches below Jirishanca’s actual summit.

When I wrote that article, some people called it a hit piece; I thought I was going light on the climbers. So when the Piolets d’Or announced their “long list” of notable ascents of 2025 earlier this year, I combed through it, seeing which routes made the cut. (Not all long-listed ascents are short-listed for the Piolets d’Or. The long list aims to recognize all new alpine climbs accomplished in good style.)

The Jirishanca “east summit” team was absent in the Peru section; the jury had not even considered their effort a route. But also within Peru’s long list, in the very same Huayhuash range, was a route on a mountain I had not heard much about, by climbers far less famous than their peers down valley. It was about the same length as the Jirishanca route, and just as difficult, but it went to the summit of Yerupajá Grande (6,635 m) and down the opposite side of the mountain. The climbers called it The Essence of Commitment, and they were being awarded the highest prize in alpinism.

As brands dive into ChatGPT, OpenAI will let them track their mentions

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If AI chatbots are going to remain a relevant touchpoint for marketers, then agencies and brands need access to the same analytics they use in their other campaigns. Rumor has it that OpenAI is developing new tools in that area, potentially creating new opportunities for advertisers on ChatGPT.

The rumor in question comes from Juozas Kaziukėnas, the founder of research firm Marketplace Pulse. On LinkedIn, Kaziukėnas posted a screenshot that shows a prompt to “claim your business in ChatGPT.” Among other perks, those who stake their claims will be able to track how often ChatGPT mentions their business within its replies.

Kaziukėnas said that he first noticed the claiming option in August, but he now believes it is close to launching. Multiple sources told Digiday that the business tracker will plot mentions and clicks on a dashboard that will depict day-to-day metrics.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

From a marketing standpoint, that’s a rudimentary level of analytics, but by showing brands their ChatGPT mentions, OpenAI can give them tangible evidence of the results of any marketing pushes they make on the platform. As answer engine optimization becomes a valuable marketing category — and the firms running AEO campaigns haul in big funding rounds — OpenAI is bringing improved measurement to that ecosystem.

If ChatGPT’s brand dashboard is indeed a play for AEO dollars, it will help answer a question that has followed the chatbot ever since its 2023 ascension: What is the best way for OpenAI to monetize its prized technology? Despite early experiments like the GPT Store, ChatGPT’s share of the LLM market has been shrinking. To bring in more revenue, OpenAI started running ads for lower-tier ChatGPT users, even though CEO Sam Altman previously expressed his distaste for that format.

Fast forward a few months, and AEO is now gaining favor. A recent report from AI search analytics platform Peec AI showed that YouTube videos show up far more frequently in chatbot responses and Google’s AI Search Overview when the descriptions of those videos receive edits. The message is clear: Tech companies want brands to play around with major chatbots, and OpenAI is equipping those brands with the metrics they need to analyze their experiments.

APOD: 2026 November 10 – Saturn Behind the Moon

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Explanation: Sometimes Saturn disappears. It doesn't really go away, of course -- it just disappears from our view when Earth's Moon moves in front. Such a Saturnian eclipse, more formally called an occultation, was visible in early 2025 along a small swath of Earth -- from Greenland through central Europe and into northern Africa. The featured image is a digital fusion of three images, the first of just Saturn before it disappeared, and then two more of the Moon with different exposure lengths. The images were taken from Sardinia, Italy, after sunset. The bright dot near Saturn is not a moon of Saturn, but rather the star 85 Aquarii, which is further behind Saturn than Saturn is behind the Moon. The next time the Moon moves in front of Saturn, as visible from some place on Earth, will be in 2031. Tomorrow's picture: small but mighty

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Avalanche [AVAX] wasn’t having a great time. Then, NYSE decided to use Avalanche to settle tokenized stocks, and AVAX woke up fast!

The timing worked out nicely too, with the platform walking out of its best quarter yet.

To put it simply, Avalanche is where the receipts will get written.

According to a press release, Avalanche will be the core ledger for the clearing firm behind an upcoming NYSE-affiliated trading platform. Every trade will get cleared and settled on it.

The stocks themselves can be issued on other blockchains.

NYSE’s own Pillar engine will still handle the trading. The plan is round-the-clock trading, instant settlement and funding with stablecoins, though it’s pending regulator approval.

Why Avalanche though? According to Dennis Lee, the man who heads digital issuance at the NYSE, the exchange wanted a ledger that settles fast, keeps costs predictable, and lets them decide who gets in.

The timing here is interesting because this NYSE news is coming along the back of Avalanche’s best Q3 record.

According to data from Blockworks and Avalanche, Pharaoh’s DEX volume went up by 2x from Q2 to Q3 to $5.33 billion. Spending on Avalanche-linked cards also went up to 74%.

The finance side went up too. On-chain credit went up by 34% to $37.4 million. Tokenized treasuries on the network hlked 14% too.

For anyone wondering if people actually want stocks on Avalanche, here’s something to note though. Dinari’s stock purchases went up 34% to 9 million. That’s small next to Wall Street, yes, but it’s the same idea the NYSE is now building towards. Just… bigger.

On top of all that, the mainnet has handled 1.6 billion transactions in total!

The altcoin spent most of the morning simply drifting around $8.00, after bottoming out near $7.83. From there, it went up fast.

In fact, its price shot up from $8.10 to $8.45, and it didn’t stop. AVAX tagged roughly $8.93 before settling near $8.83. That hinted at a 12.7% hike above the altcoin’s daily low.

Its RSI shot past 90 during the spike, before falling to 62.5 at press time. That was just below the Signal Line.

Finally, the DMI seemed to be bit more convincing. The ADX was at 56.7, hinting at a strong trend. Buyers seemed to be leading the charge, with +DI at 29.7 against -DI at 13.1.

If AVAX can push through $9, things are likely to get interesting.

Autumn Mornings Bring Valley Fog

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Low clouds trace the branching valleys of the Appalachian Plateau and the Cumberland River, then burn off by afternoon.

On a cool November morning, low clouds traced the branching valleys of the Appalachian Plateau and the Cumberland River. By afternoon, they had faded, leaving clear skies over the autumn landscape.

The change is visible in this pair of images from November 8, 2026. The MODIS (Moderate Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer) on NASA's Terra satellite acquired the left image that morning. In this natural-color view, low clouds appear white as they fill many of the valleys. Autumn vegetation appears in shades of brown and green.

The VIIRS (Visible Infrared Imaging Radiometer Suite) on NOAA-21 acquired the right image that afternoon, after the clouds had dissipated and exposed the rivers and lakes beneath them. This false-color view makes it easier to distinguish water (dark blue) from vegetation (bright green).

The clouds appear to be fog, a type of cloud that forms near the ground. Foggy mornings in the region are common this time of year. As nights lengthen, the ground has more time to radiate away the day's heat, and the air just above it cools to the dew point—the temperature at which air becomes saturated and water vapor begins condensing into the tiny droplets that make up fog.

Because cold air is denser than warm air, it flows downslope and pools in valleys, so fog tends to form there first. Notice the fog filling the branching valleys across part of the Appalachian Plateau in eastern Kentucky and southwestern West Virginia. Rivers and streams that run along many of the valley floors add to the effect by supplying extra moisture. The same pattern is visible along the Cumberland River and its reservoirs in north-central Tennessee and south-central Kentucky.

According to the National Weather Service, temperatures in many places on the morning of November 8 dipped into the 30s Fahrenheit before giving way to a mild, sunny afternoon. Forecasters reported locally dense valley fog that morning. Dense fog had also blanketed a wider part of the region earlier in the week, prompting advisories for hazardous travel conditions.

NASA Earth Observatory images by Lauren Dauphin, using MODIS data from NASA EOSDIS LANCE and GIBS/Worldview, and VIIRS data from NASA EOSDIS LANCE, GIBS/Worldview and the Joint Polar Satellite System (JPSS). Story by Kathryn Hansen.

Sunlight, Weather and Childcare: A Year of New Data on Redfin Home Listings

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In 2026, Redfin added information about sunlight, weather, childcare access and more to home listings. Here is everything you need to know about what’s new.

A home listing has always been good at telling you about a house: the square footage, the year it was built, the number of bedrooms. Historically, it has been less good at telling you what living there is actually like. Does the kitchen get morning sunlight? How hot are the summers? Is there a daycare within walking distance?

Those are the questions we set out to answer this year. Multiple new data sets arrived on Redfin in 2026, and they now sit alongside other information that was already there, including scores about a property’s walkability, transit access and climate.

Sunscore shows exactly how much sunlight a home gets

Natural light is one of the hardest things to judge from listing photos or a single showing, and it matters more than you might expect. In a recent Redfin survey, nearly 70% of people said the amount of sunlight in their home affects their satisfaction with where they live, and 11% said it is non-negotiable when searching for a home.

Sunscore is a property-level rating from 0 to 100 that shows how much sunlight a home gets. We co-created it with Shadowmap, a sunlight analysis platform, using 3D modeling that tracks how shadows fall across a home throughout the day and across the seasons. Here are the score breakdowns:

Each score accounts for the home’s 3D shape and structure, surrounding buildings, trees and terrain, the sun’s position throughout the day, and seasonal changes across the year. In other words, it catches the things you would only discover after moving in—like a neighboring building that blocks the afternoon light in winter.

And it is not only about comfort and aesthetics. A home’s sun exposure also says something about its solar potential, which can translate into real savings on energy costs over time.

Weather data helps predict precipitation and heat

Weather turns out to be one of the biggest reasons people move. In a recent Redfin survey conducted by Ipsos, 22% of Americans planning to move to another state said better weather was motivating the move—the most cited reason out of nearly 30 options, ahead of affordability, job opportunities and moving closer to family. Another 21% said concerns about climate change or natural disasters were shaping where they wanted to live.

In July, we partnered with The Weather Company, owner of The Weather Channel app and weather.com, to bring historical weather data to every for-sale listing on Redfin. As our exclusive historical weather provider, it supplies the same data hundreds of millions of people check every month.

Each listing shows ZIP code-level averages for:

Consider this information the difference between the weather on the day you tour a home and the climate you might actually live in.

Childcare data displays daycare and preschool options

For families with young children, childcare access can determine a neighborhood’s fit as much as school boundaries do. However, it’s historically been missing from the home browsing experience. Redfin is the first major real estate portal to partner with Winnie, the largest marketplace for childcare and early education in the U.S., to bring local childcare information to for-sale listings.

Nearby daycare and preschool options now appear on for-sale home listings with details that determine whether a spot is realistic for your family, such as:

Location scores capture everything from wellness access to noise

While Walk Score, Bike Score and Transit Score already existed on home listings, Redfin has expanded these location scores to capture more information about a neighborhood’s vibe using Local Logic data.

Start exploring homes on Redfin.com or the Redfin app to learn everything you’d want to know about a property. Need a place to start? Use Redfin’s conversational search to begin your homebuying journey.

Nazca infant had rare, fatal birth defect that left her with one central eye 1,600 years ago

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A DNA analysis has confirmed that a Nazca infant who lived in southern Peru 1,600 years ago had a rare congenital condition called trisomy 13.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Archaeologists in Peru have discovered the skeleton of an infant with a rare birth defect that left only one central eye socket in the child's skull. DNA analysis of the remains confirmed a chromosomal abnormality caused the deadly condition in the infant over 1,600 years ago, according to a new study.

"We cannot really say how long this individual survived," study co-author Lars Fehren-Schmitz, an anthropologist at the University of California, Santa Cruz, told Live Science in an email. But "the burial of this infant suggests that the Nasca treated this individual in death like most other infants."

The skeleton of this female infant was discovered alongside a second one inside a ceramic urn at the archaeological site of Jauranga in Peru's Palpa Valley, where numerous geoglyphs known as the Nazca Lines have been found. Archaeologists uncovered several dozen other burials dated to the Middle Nazca (also spelled Nasca) period (A.D. 325 to 440) at the site, but one of the co-buried infants had an extremely rare condition, researchers wrote in a study published Monday (Nov. 9) in the journal PNAS.

The infant had a very small skull and a single eye socket in the middle of her face, along with other developmental issues of the cranial bones. These traits match modern clinical descriptions of cyclopia, a lethal condition that occurs when the front part of a fetus's brain fails to divide into left and right hemispheres.

This condition can also cause alterations in the nasal structures, resulting in a missing nose or a tube-like nose above the single eye.

A medical illustration from 1891 of two infants with cyclopia.

"The cause of the malformation was present at fertilization, but manifestation occurs around the fourth to fifth week in utero [in the womb], when the frontal lobe of the brain has to divide," study first author Elsa Tomasto-Cagigao, an archaeologist at the Pontifical Catholic University of Peru, told Live Science in an email.

Cyclopia is a rare condition, occurring in about 1 out of every 100,000 births. To learn more about the infant's short life, the researchers sequenced her genome. (DNA suggested the infant carried two X chromosomes, typical of a female.)

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

The team detected an extra copy of chromosome 13, meaning the infant had a genetic condition called trisomy 13, or Patau syndrome. It's known to be a major chromosomal cause of cyclopia; that said, only a small percentage of trisomy 13 cases involve cyclopia.

While trisomy 13 is associated with advanced maternal age, it is not clear whether that was the case for this infant, Fehren-Schmitz said.

"This condition has been documented in historic and modern anatomical collections, but to my knowledge this is the first archaeological example of this developmental abnormality," Siân Halcrow, a bioarchaeologist at the University of Otago in New Zealand who was not involved in the study, told Live Science in an email.

The researchers' estimate of the infant's age at death suggests she may have survived for several weeks or months after birth, Halcrow pointed out. "This raises important questions about how infants with profound congenital differences were cared for and treated in past societies," she said.

The infant was buried in a ceramic urn, the researchers noted in the study, which was a common burial method in the Nazca period, along with a second female infant who had no skeletal abnormalities. "The baby with cyclopia was at the bottom of the jar, while the other baby was on top. Nevertheless, it appears that they were interred in a single event," Tomasto-Cagigao said. The genetic analysis showed that the two infants did not have the same mother.

A burial of two infants in one urn is unusual for this culture, the researchers said, but there is not enough archaeological evidence to conclude that the infant with cyclopia was given a different type of burial.

"The fact that the individual was buried with a 'healthy' individual in a regular cemetery, without any special grave goods, suggests that, to some extent, the individual was not treated differently in death, even though it had such a rare condition," Fehren-Schmitz said. "It was neither 'excluded' because of it nor treated in a 'special' way."

In ancient Andean societies, some people with disabilities took on special societal roles, such as ones associated with religious practices. "If that reflects Nasca society as a whole, we can't say based on one case," Fehren-Schmitz said, "but it does reflect the community this infant was part of, even if only briefly."

Tomasto-Cagigao, E., Reindel, M., Isla Cuadrado, J., Lund Valle, M., Kassadjikova, K., Harkins, K., Fehren-Schmitz, L. (2026). A Nasca-period perinatal burial reveals cyclopia associated with trisomy 13 in southern Peru. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. https://www.pnas.org/cgi/doi/10.1073/pnas.2625042123

Kristina Killgrove is a staff writer at Live Science with a focus on archaeology and paleoanthropology news. Her articles have also appeared in venues such as Forbes, Smithsonian, and Mental Floss. Kristina holds a Ph.D. in biological anthropology and an M.A. in classical archaeology from the University of North Carolina, as well as a B.A. in Latin from the University of Virginia, and she was formerly a university professor and researcher. She has received awards from the Society for American Archaeology and the American Anthropological Association for her science writing.

Top 50 Most Subscribed YouTube Channels Worldwide • Week Of 11/08/2026

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

[Editor’s Note: Tubefilter Charts is a weekly rankings column from Tubefilter with data provided by GospelStats. It’s exactly what it sounds like; a top number ranking of YouTube channels based on statistics collected within a given time frame. Check out all of our Tubefilter Charts with new installments every week right here.]

Scroll down for this week’s Tubefilter Chart. 👇

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

Though a creator based in Turkey is dominating the Global Sub Top 50, India is still the most ubiquitous country in the ranking. 14 of the top 50 channels hail from that nation, and a few of them have discovered a science experiment that reliably delivers millions of views each day.

Want to feel old? This video arrived on YouTube more than two decades ago.

Once upon a time, the combination of Diet Coke and Mentos produced a viral online video trend. Millions of viewers tuned in as amateur scientists performed backyard experiments, turning ordinary soda bottles into towering fountains of bubbling spray.

The Diet Coke and Mentos trend has survived into the era of YouTube Shorts, but it’s not nearly the fad it once was. Instead, creators have found another combination that produces a terrific reaction: ENO antacids and water.

ENO tablets are similar to Alka-Seltzer, in that they are effervescent antacids capable of curing tummy troubles. The products differ in their active ingredients and the regions where they are most commonly consumed. ENO is a common drugstore find across South Asia.

Some Indian creators have figured out a trick that turns a humble ENO tablet into an improvised firecracker. By injecting water directly into ENO packages, those creators produce localized reactions that cause the packets to explode after a few seconds.

Here’s what makes the ENO videos extra intriguing: The channels uploading these clips are including shoppable links that viewers can use to pick up the tablets for themselves. It’s as if the Diet Coke and Mentos channels from back in the day had sold those products right there on the screen (which, let’s be honest, if YouTube had its shopping tools back then, there definitely would have been some in-app Mentos purchases going on).

The ENO trend’s commercial ties give it legs. Creators aren’t just encouraged to try out this experiment because it gets them views. They can also profit from it directly, and the homemade firecrackers only take a few seconds to set up.

And if you don’t live in a region where ENO tablets are available, well, maybe it’s time to bring back Diet Coke and Mentos. Everything old on the internet eventually becomes new again, and as one of YouTube’s oldest trends comes back around, the creators peddling it can even work in some sales, too.

Samsung Galaxy S27 series camera specs leak, and they’re disappointing

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

New leaks reveal the camera specs for Samsung’s upcoming Galaxy S27 series which includes three lenses across the lineup, but not the upgrades you want, nor what Ultra owners might expect.

SammyGuru reports that Samsung is “evaluating” camera setups for the Galaxy S27, S27+, S27 Pro, and S27 Ultra, and it looks like a mixed bag across the board.

The base Galaxy S27 and S27+ are looking very familiar, with identical main camera sensors to last year, and the same ultrawide sensor that’s been in use since the Galaxy S23 series. The telephoto and selfie sensors are looking at new hardware, but without any major spec upgrades.

Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, meanwhile, have a new 200MP main camera on deck, a 50MP ultrawide shooter, and a mere 12MP telephoto. The Ultra might get 5x compared to the Pro’s 3x optical zoom, but with a secondary telephoto lens going missing, existing users of the lineup are sure to be disappointed.

Notably, SammyGuru’s article mentions the Pro as using “the Galaxy S26 Ultra’s 3x sensor,” but shows the 12MP Samsung S5K3LD in that place instead. We’re assuming that’s a typo seeing as the post mentions the Pro as using the Samsung S5KJN3 or the Sony IMX855 earlier on, but regardless, the Pro only gets 3x optical zoom to the Ultra’s 5x.

Some of these specs have been mentioned by other sources such as GalaxyClub and leaker Ice Universe, but this is the most complete picture of the S27’s camera plans we’ve seen to date.

Personally, I’m very “whelmed” on this one. As RAMageddon rages on and Samsung hunts for new ways to balance specs vs price, cameras are a place that’s obviously going to be cut, but competitors are already running laps around Samsung on the hardware front. Cutting lenses and skimping out on upgrades (again) doesn’t help matters.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Let’s worry about the septuagenarians and octogenarians later. For now, let’s grab some popcorn and watch them get to work.

Waiter? I'll take everything on the menu.

Reporter Mona Burns has put together a list of possible committee chairmen once the new Democratic Senate convenes.

1) This list is a bit heavy on septuagenarians like, well, me.

2) The GOP already is hip-deep in a leadership brawl that almost perfectly parallels the struggle over who was to blame for the bloodbath.

I'll worry about my age cohort later. For now, I'll put my feet up and enjoy the fact that Senator Professor Warren is already on the case. There is not enough popcorn in the world.

Follow One Rule For Crispier Chicken Skin Every Time (Thanks, Jacques Pépin)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Crispy skin needs dry heat, a bit of fat, and plenty of time to develop the beautiful golden-brown coloring that always signifies great flavor. But not every recipe can achieve this. That's why Jacques Pépin has a simple rule: Only leave the skin on if you're serving the chicken browned.

Skin preserves moisture in meat, which is why it's a key component of Ina Garten's secret for juicier chicken. Under dry heat, the fat just below its surface renders, infusing the meat with unparalleled deliciousness while the skin becomes crispy and rich. For Pépin's famous poulet à la crème, or chicken with cream sauce, however, he prefers to remove the skin because he says moisture replaces the fat, making it gummy and unappetizing (via YouTube). This is largely because moist cooking methods soften the skin and prevent it from crisping, which has its place in the kitchen but isn't great for a straightforward piece of roasted chicken.

If you're ever in doubt, always think about the end product. Pépin has no problem browning meat first to add some Maillard flavor to the sauce, but you can do this just fine if you remove the skin first. Not knowing when to remove the skin is an especially detrimental mistake to avoid when making chicken thighs, which naturally contain more fat and have great potential for crispy skin. A moisture-free, dry environment is vital to getting the right result, so pat them dry beforehand and keep them dry when cooking if you want that extra-crispy skin.

Even if you don't serve it on the meat itself, skin has a ton of potential in a wide variety of recipes. Whether you have yellow or white chicken skin, from the breast or thigh, large or small, each scrap is a valuable source of potential flavor you can repurpose for other foods or use to make future ingredients.

If you want something easy and long-lasting, there's no better use for chicken skin than for stocks. While you'll still want to include some backbones or feet to round out the flavor, this scrap comes loaded with fat and collagen that don't need a ton of time to render into the water. While you may not have enough from one recipe, you can always chuck it in a zip-top bag and freeze it for up to nine months while you build your "stock" pile. This is especially good if you have a surplus of tiny scraps that may not be as useful in other recipes by themselves.

If you have a few larger pieces of skin, these are prime candidates for cracklings. Jacques Pépin's own mother used to bake them in the oven for about 20 to 30 minutes at 400 degrees Fahrenheit for something he says is better than bacon and a perfect topping for salads. If you want to get fancy with it, you can wrap small, precooked foods, like meatballs or mushrooms, in them and roast them in the oven for little chicken skin lollipops!
