1961 Chevy Impala Bubble Top For Sale With 1,111 Miles Shown Is A Wild LS V8 Restomod

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

When GM styling head Bill Mitchell set about redesigning the Chevrolet look for the 1961 model year, taking over from the iconic Harley Earl, he wanted to make a big shift away from the upright aesthetic with dramatic tail fins that defined the brand's 1950s. Moving into a smoother and cleaner jet age look, the 1961 Impala looked lower, longer, wider, and more streamlined. With wire-thin pillars and wraparound windows front and rear, the unique Sport Coupe model came to be known as a "bubbletop" in enthusiast circles. Decades on from its production date, this Impala design looks as beautiful as the day it was born. This particular example, currently for sale with a bubble-bursting $259,900 price tag, hides some modern improvements to make it an even better Impala.

Many car fanatics drive vintage cars because they're stylistically impressive, and it doesn't get much more impressive than a bubble top Impala scraping the ground on bags. There are downsides to driving a classic every day, though. Old cars typically don't have the modern amenities that American drivers have come to expect, like the unstoppable power of a fuel-injected small-block Chevrolet V8, a modern overdrive transmission, the stopping power of disc brakes, or modern air suspension. Thankfully, this Impala is fitted with all of these. This is a showstopper that was built to handle the rigors of modern traffic. What more could you want?

Take one part old Impala, one part modern Corvette, and one part creative hot-rod ingenuity, and you get a magnificent piece of automotive art. Albeit automotive art with the price tag of a long-lost Gertrude Abercrombie surrealist masterpiece. I want to know everything there is to know about this machine, and I bet you do, too.

While the car still carries all of the beautiful bodywork of a '61 Impala, it appears that just about everything under the skin has been replaced with modern alternatives. With new drivetrain components, suspension, steering, and brakes, this looks like a truly incredible driving experience waiting to happen. With the seller claiming just 1,111 miles on the odometer, it's just barely been broken in and should be taken on a nice long road trip with expediency. Imagine next summer cruising back and forth across the country on Route 66 in a black-over-red Impala and barely breaking a sweat because you have a nice fuel-efficient LS engine, graceful power steering, comfortable suspension, and ice-cold Vintage Air A/C blowing. Yeah, that's the American dream.

Back in 1961, Impala buyers would have been given three different engines to choose from, including a Blue Flame six-cylinder, a 283ci V8, and a 348ci V8. The most powerful Impala of its day featured 350 horsepower from a "Special Super Turbo-Thrust" V8 with three two-barrel carburetors. A modern LS2, cribbed from a C6 Corvette, makes an uncomplicated 400 ponies from its six-liter displacement. Presumably for the sake of simplicity and availability, the shop decided to pair this engine with a 4L60 four-speed automatic. This was an extremely common transmission, used in practically every rear-wheel drive General Motors product built from the mid-1990s to about 2010. This car should have no trouble delivering the giddyup, or laying some rubber if you want it to.

The seller describes the suspension setup thusly:

Equipped with a complete Ridetech Air Ride Suspension System, this Impala can transform from a pavement-hugging showpiece to a comfortable highway cruiser at the touch of a button!

Inside the Impala, there's an interesting juxtaposition of old and new, with gorgeous period-correct red interior upholstery wrapped around custom bucket seats, a unique center console, and plenty of retro-modern touches, like the full Dakota Digital gauge cluster. In addition to the modern air conditioning, this car is fitted with a modern (unspecified) multi-speaker sound system. I'm not sure how much music you'll need, considering the LS2's soundtrack is bolstered with a stainless-steel Borla exhaust.

I don't like the Billet Specialties wheels, and I think they're much too large for this car, but a nice set of 18s wouldn't go amiss here. Likewise, the steering wheel is a touch too far. Beyond that, I'm not sure there is much I would amend here. It's a nice car that appears to be nicely done. I'd want a few thousand more miles of testing on a completely custom car before taking it on a big trip, but the parts to fix it will be available at whatever parts counter you find along Route 66.

I'm not going to try to justify the price tag, because this is an absolutely absurd number, and I don't think they'll get anyone to pay it. If you have the spare quarter mil, though, there are a lot worse cars you could buy. Anything new in that price range is going to be faster, but will it be as effortlessly stylish? What's the point in buying a new Porsche 911 GT3 when a sea of them parks at your local cars and coffee? You could instead arrive in something that has show-worthy style, and you'd still have enough comfort to cross the country.

Mona Chalabi Paints a Picture of Class Inequality

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

“The crisis is the system that we’re in,” the Pulitzer Prize-winning data journalist and artist says in an interview with Hyperallegic about her first book, “Ten Lives.”

“As an adult and as a journalist, I have stared shamelessly.” That refusal to look away is precisely what data journalist, writer, and artist Mona Chalabi has built her deeply principled career on. Known best for her indelible data visualizations, from the obscenity of Jeff Bezos’s wealth to the Israeli government’s starvation of Palestinians in Gaza and the racial and gender biases in museum collections, the Pulitzer Prize-winning Iraqi-British journalist tackles the subject of wealth and economic inequality in America in her first book, Ten Lives: How What We Have Shapes Who We Are (2026).

When I met her in the West Village in September, she’d just come from recording its accompanying audiobook, a delicate process given the visuals on its pages. Chalabi organized meticulous interviews conducted with anonymous subjects across income levels over the course of six years into 10 vividly fleshed-out characters. Each represents a particular economic class, illustrated in her signature hand-drawn style and punctuated by reflections on her own financial struggles as a young journalist and, more recently, in the wake of her mother’s illness. Chalabi carries a long tradition of data visualization — W.E.B. Du Bois’s The Exhibit of American Negroes comes to mind — into her refreshing candor about class and the stifling silence around it, particularly in journalism and the art world.

That insistence on specificity and honesty in her reporting goes hand-in-hand with her political advocacy; Chalabi donated her $15,000 Pulitzer Prize award to the Palestinian Journalists Syndicate and has been fighting for Palestinian rights since she was a teenager. Her drive to seek out and represent truth, in Ten Lives and in her social media posts, is anchored in a sensitivity and profound sense of responsibility. In this way, she restores some of the humanity and dignity to the statistics that capture the brutality of genocide, exploitation, and economic inequality, one brushstroke at a time. In an interview, we spoke about the financial realities of journalism, the value of collaboration in an era of isolation, and the art and literature that sustains her. Our conversation has been lightly edited for clarity and length.

Hyperallergic: In the intro to Ten Lives, you recall getting scolded for not observing “the etiquette of looking away” as a child. And it’s so striking that you have made a career of doing the opposite. How did you decide to make class, something we’re taught to overlook from a young age, the subject of this book?

Mona Chalabi: The first line of the original manuscript said, “I wrote this book because I was paid a shitload of money to write it.”

MC: And my editor was like, “Yeah, you can't do that. No one's gonna want to read a book that you wrote simply for money.” But I also knew that this was a commercial idea, and that did inform it. I have been quite shameless about the fact that I want my work to reach a wide audience. That has always, always mattered to me. It was the beginning of the pandemic, and part of the way that I pitched it was this idea that there's gonna be a big financial crisis and the dynamics are more nuanced than simply talking about the one percent. There's this idea of a coming economic collapse or a coming financial crisis. But as soon as we're in it, it's not this cyclical model. The crisis is the system that we're in that is so broken.

H: That gradation of class that usually doesn't get talked about is such a crucial contribution of this book. What led you to stratify these stories based on subcategories that often just get collapsed into “working class, middle class, upper class”?

MC: Class is really informative about who I am and how I move through the world and my relationships. And I felt like it was really, really lacking in our discourse. I feel a bit hesitant to use the word “discourse,” but it's just true. And it's not just about discourse, it's more mundane than that. It's the way that someone like me can say, “I'm an Arab, cis, heterosexual woman,” with total ease, but class doesn't really come into it. There's a dishonesty there. Actually, class is really, really important. And many of us aren't really talking about that in accurate and fruitful ways.

10 Hacks Every Waze User Should Know

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City. After graduating from Duke University, she spent several years reporting on the federal workforce for Government Executive, a publication of Atlantic Media Company, in Washington, D.C. She has nearly a decade of experience as a freelancer covering tech (including issues related to security, privacy, and streaming) as well as personal finance and travel.

In addition to Lifehacker, her work has been featured on Wirecutter, Tom’s Guide, and ZDNET. Emily has also worked as a travel guide around the U.S. and as a content editor. She has a masters in social work and is a licensed therapist in Utah.

Waze is a crowdsourced navigation app designed to help you get where you're going as quickly and with as much information about your route as possible. Where Google Maps and Apple Maps offer relatively straightforward directions, Waze tries to outsmart these alternatives by integrating real-time community feedback about hazards, speed traps, road closures, and more. If you are switching to Waze or using it alongside your other navigation tools, these are the hacks to help you maximize everything the app has to offer.

If you find yourself losing track of time or running late for events, Waze can notify you when it's time to leave—based on your current location and live traffic—so you arrive on schedule. You can set up "Time to leave" reminders in advance for individual planned drives, or you can sync your calendar to receive notifications for scheduled events. Calendar sync connects to your device's default calendar app, but you can change this to a third-party alternative if you prefer. Calendar events must include a navigable address to trigger reminders, and you must select Plan a drive in Waze for the event.

Waze has a feature called "child reminders," which are notifications to check for children before you get out of the car. This is clearly an incredibly important use case, but you can also use these alerts for custom messages, like "Don't forget the groceries!" In Waze, tap the menu button > Settings > Reminders > Child reminder and select Custom message.

Variable speed limits, especially on unfamiliar routes, can result in you speeding when you don't mean to. But Waze can alert you if you exceed a customized speed threshold so you aren't caught by surprise or at higher risk of being ticketed. From the menu, go to Settings > Speedometer and turn on Show speedometer. Tap Speeding threshold to select what limit you want to trigger alerts, and turn on Alert at threshold if you want Waze to play a sound.

Waze offers a wealth of information on routes to help drivers navigate obstacles and hazards, but this can clutter your map and distract you from the most critical information, especially in congested, high-traffic areas. Waze says it limits and prioritizes alerts automatically, but you can actually customize which alerts you see based on what's most important to you. From the menu, go to Settings > Alerts and reports > Reports and select the relevant alerts.

In the same Alerts and reports menu, you can enable Alert while driving, which will give you an audio warning and an onscreen pop-up about your selected hazards. This allows you to run navigation via a different map app (like Google Maps or Apple Maps) in the foreground while still getting Waze data in the background.

Waze integrates with toll road and HOV lane passes, so if you have an express or fast lane subscription, your route will optimize for it. If you don't, Waze can still account for HOV lane eligibility if you tell it how many passengers are in your car. From the menu, go to Settings > Toll and HOV passes and tap the plus icon to add your pass(es) from the list. If you need to add your passenger count instead, you can do so from View routes and enable the HOV option.

If you're driving through an unfamiliar, highly congested, or poorly designed area, you can ask Waze to help you avoid tricky maneuvers, such as crossing multiple lanes of traffic or turning left at intersections without lights. This prioritizes ease over speed using protected green arrows, roundabouts, and right turns. From the menu, go to Settings > Navigation, Avoid difficult intersections and select Always. You can also ask Waze to avoid toll roads, ferries, freeways, and unpaved roads.

Waze has a built-in audio player that syncs with your preferred listening platform—such as Spotify, YouTube Music, and Pandora—so you can control your music without having to navigate between apps. (As Waze is a Google product, Apple Music isn't included.) Simply tap the music icon on the map to select your audio player and hit Accept. If you don't see the icon, go to Settings > Audio player and turn on Show on the map.

If you want to make audio directions more interesting, you can select a celebrity or character voice to help you navigate. Recent appearances include Bill Hader's Cat in the Hat and Ziggy Marley. Tap the speaker icon > Current voice to search for the voice or language you want to use. You can also record directions in your own voice.

If you don't want Waze storing certain types of data—or if you want to keep your activity hidden from other users—you can adjust various privacy settings in the app. You will certainly want to turn off personalized ads (under Settings > Privacy) and consider enabling invisible mode, which keeps your username, mood, photo, and online status hidden from other Waze users. Finally, you can delete recent locations and drives as well as your navigation history at any time.

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City.

Winners of the Banff Mountain Film Festival Announced

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The annual Banff Centre Mountain Festival gathers mountain luminaries from across the world at the Banff Centre for Arts and Creativity in Alberta. There, a celebration of mountain culture recognizes the best outdoor films and books of the past year. The climax of the festival took place this past weekend, and the winners of the 51st annual Banff Mountain Film Competition have now been announced.

The Grand Prize went to Listen, directed by Taliesin Black-Brown. In this short documentary, sound engineer Hank Lentfer records the sounds of nature around his Alaska home, while caring for his mother with dementia. As Lentfer documents the degradation of the natural world, we witness the slow loss of his mother. Drawing together these two threads, the film explores connection despite, and through, loss, using sound.

Rose Bush, an Oscar Award-winning director of photography serving on the competition’s jury, called Listen “a film that engages human-caused environmental devastation — the existential dilemma of our time — at its nucleus with overwhelming love and hope.”

The film also won the Grand Jury Prize at the Tribeca Film Festival.

In addition to a grand prize, the festival honors films in several categories. The winning film in the Adventure category was The Navigator, a feature-length work from director Jordan Manley. The film follows Nainoa Thompson, who was the first Native Hawaiian in six centuries to make the journey from Hawaii to Tahiti in a traditional voyaging canoe. Now, almost five decades later, Thompson and his crew make the journey again, using only traditional navigation techniques.

The best film in the Mountain Culture category was Land of Canaan, produced and directed by Maggie Lemere. Lemere’s film follows a group of Palestinian olive farmers, who work to protect their ancient olive groves amid the destruction by Israel. The film “illuminated the vital relationship of courage, vision, hope, and traditional ecological knowledge,” juror Rose Bush said, “in service of the essential value of sovereignty via mountain culture.”

In the Environment category, director James Dawson’s winning comedic documentary Derek vs Derek follows two Devon farmers, both named Derek. One Derek is an ardent dairy farmer while his environmentalist neighbor begins rewilding his land. A feud naturally ensues.

The Arc’teryx-sponsored Best Climbing Film was Zindabad, directed by Tom Fish. Fish follows Pakistani climber Mueez Ud Din into the Karakoram, alongside his uncle Javed Ali and French climber Mathieu Maynadier. Ud Din explores a remote, unknown glacier system, while the film examines what it means to be a Pakistani climber among peaks long claimed by international outsiders.

Directed by Alexis Berg, the Mountain Sports winner, Roots, follows trail runner Ruth Croft as she examines her future as an elite ultra-trail runner after an injury. Roots is “about an inner journey of healing and acceptance, rooted in the essential joy of running outdoors,” explained juror David Arnaud.

The Snow Sports winner centers on American skier Jessie Diggins. Diggins is the most decorated American cross-country skier ever, but Threshold, directed by Torsten Brinkema, reveals the deep personal struggles behind that success. Juror Beki Henderson noted that “the jury particularly commends Jessie Diggins for her extraordinary courage and honesty in sharing her story.”

There were more mountain films this year than you can even imagine. With 610 film submissions and 83 finalists, it’s no wonder that there are still more category winners.

Best short film winner Hello, Beautiful follows climber Alannah Yip as she struggles with identity, training for the Olympics while enduring medical hair loss. Director Darcy Hennessey created a film which “reminds us that personal transformation does not always come from pushing harder,” juror Kerry Stauffer explained.

For best feature film, the competition announced Ceremony, directed and produced by Banchi Hanuse. The film tells the story of the disappearance of the ooligan, a small fish, from British Columbia’s Bella Coola River. Jurors praised the film’s use of watercolor animation, personal stories, and community broadcasts to explore the importance of a seemingly unimportant little fish.

Director Marco Tribelhorn’s 75 seconds won the Creative Excellence Award. The film documents free-skier Jacob Wester’s 75-second-long battle for survival while trapped in an avalanche.

“A compelling visual treatment brings to life an experience that is impossible to imagine,” said Kerry Stauffer.

Last but not least, the Audience Choice Award went to Only Music // Snake River. Director Justin Fountain’s documentary follows a band that ditches the tour bus for a journey down the Snake River, seeking connection with strangers.

Tickets for the international film festival are now available. We have also covered the recently announced Banff Mountain Book Award winners.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

“These Aren’t Victimless Crimes.” Vandals Deface Ancient Petroglyphs Near Moab.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Vandals scratched graffiti above ancient animal petroglyphs at the Bear Panel along Highway 279 near Moab, Utah. (Photo: BLM Utah)

The Bureau of Land Management (BLM) announced on November 6 that it was investigating vandalism at two ancient rock-art sites near Moab, Utah, where someone carved names into petroglyphs, leaving damage that may be impossible to remove.

The incidents occurred at the Bear Panel, a rock-art site along Highway 279 near the Colorado River, and another panel near Hell’s Revenge, a popular four-wheel-drive trail in the Sand Flats Recreation Area.

“These images are a physical expression of the past, their connection to their ancestors and to that landscape,” Chris Merritt, Utah’s state historic preservation officer, told Outside. “They will now forever be scarred by someone’s thoughtlessness, and that hurts the continuation of culture.”

The BLM has not publicly identified any suspects in the two latest incidents. An agency spokesperson told the Moab newspaper The Times-Independent that the agency planned to treat the graffiti in the days following its November 6 announcement, although some damage may be permanent.

Under the federal Archaeological Resources Protection Act, vandalizing protected archaeological sites can carry fines of up to $20,000 and two years in prison.

Photos show the name “Meru” scratched beside ancient carvings depicting a bear and a bighorn sheep at the Bear Panel. At the second site, near Hell’s Revenge, someone carved “Craig W” across several petroglyph figures.

Merritt said much of the artwork at the two sites dates back roughly 1,500 years and reflects Ancestral Puebloan and Fremont traditions, along with those of more recent Indigenous communities. The panels are part of a larger cultural landscape around Moab, where Indigenous peoples once lived, farmed, and traveled, he said.

Restoring the damaged petroglyphs presents different challenges at each site, Merritt said.

At the site near Hell’s Revenge, vandals scratched graffiti directly across ancient carvings. Merritt noted that typical fixes, such as scrubbing the stone or trying to conceal the marks with pigments, risk damaging the original artwork underneath.

The Bear Panel presents a different problem. A crack in the sandstone just below the graffiti means that scraping the rock could create openings for rainwater, weakening the structure through freeze-thaw cycles.

“Five minutes of damage could take weeks of repair to do carefully to preserve the subsurface imagery and the rock face,” Merritt said, emphasizing that any remediation must involve consultation with descendant tribal communities.

Merritt said rock-art vandalism has become more widespread in Utah in recent years, a problem he believes is partly connected to growing visitation on public lands.

“We’ve always had folks going back to the 19th century scraping their initials on the panels, but what we’re seeing within five or six years is a pretty significant increase in the scale and distribution, hitting archaeological panels that have never had graffiti before,” Merritt said.

The latest incidents follow another case in March 2026, when the Grand County Sheriff’s Office investigated graffiti carved into petroglyphs near Tusher Tunnel, along an off-highway vehicle route northwest of Moab.

To combat the problem, Merritt said Utah is using its Protect Our Past education campaign and a statewide network of volunteer site stewards who monitor archaeological sites. Signage and fencing can also help discourage vandalism, he said.

In November 2025, a Utah woman who vandalized petroglyphs on BLM land near Wire Pass was sentenced to 12 months of probation and ordered to pay $14,853 in fines and restitution, according to a U.S. Department of Justice news release. She was also prohibited from entering BLM land during her probation.

“These aren’t victimless crimes,” Merritt said. “We need to find ways to catch, prosecute, and convict these folks, so it sends a message to future visitors that this is not acceptable in Utah or anywhere you visit heritage sites.”

Anyone with information is asked to contact Moab BLM Law Enforcement at 435-259-2131.

→ Baca artikel asli

Scott Arfield lifts lid on East Fife appointment and Rangers mentors he will lean on as Steven Gerrard reacts

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The wisdom of anyone with ambition picking Bayview as a platform is spelled out to them as soon as they walk in through the front door of East Fife’s stadium.

The club wasted little time jumping on the fact Mikel Oyarzabal once featured in a friendly at Methil when the Spaniard became a world champion this summer. “Methil to the Mundial” runs the heading above two photographs in the reception area. One is of a then teenage Oyarzabal appearing for Real Sociedad in a friendly against St Johnstone played at the stadium in 2015. The other is the striker lifting the World Cup after the 1-0 victory over Argentina in New Jersey.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

"Choose Methil, conquer the world" is the less than subliminal message, one that is surely appealing to a young go-ahead manager such as Scott Arfield. East Fife have returned to a modus operandi stretching back to their late 1940s glory years of giving bright young coaches a chance.

Like then, when the 36-year-old Scot Symon was recruited, Arfield is a former Rangers player still in his 30s who is desperate to take his first steps in management.

The landscape is rather different now although East Fife has continued to be a fertile place for budding young managers, Gary Naysmith among them. One of his Scotland jerseys hangs in the Legends Lounge, on which Naysmith has written: “To East Fife FC, Thank you for my first managerial role”. Maybe in time Arfield, who played 20 times for Canada, will gift one of his old international shirts if he goes on to bigger and better things.

Right now he is simply concentrating on learning his new players’ names and getting himself match fit. The 38-year-old’s last match as a player was towards the end of last season for Livingston against Aberdeen. Arfield took over as manager on a caretaker basis following Marvin Bartley’s departure and restricted himself to a watching role.

This won’t happen at East Fife, although he will require a few weeks to get up to speed. The League One club, currently sitting sixth, have bagged a very decent player into the bargain. This wasn’t the case when they appointed his predecessor Dick Campbell, who was 70 at the time.

Slightly surprisingly, Arfield has never met his legendary predecessor. “I have never crossed paths with Dick,” he said. “He is the one figure I did want to come across and didn’t.

“I have met loads of players throughout my career who have great stories about him. Listen, we have probably got different ideas about how the game should be played. But there is one ball and two goals…”

That’s not to say Arfield has something against those perceived to be from the old school. One of his mentors, who he says he still speaks to regularly, is John Hughes, who was manager when Arfield was breaking through with Falkirk. “I have known him since I was 15 and he is giving me great pointers,” he says. “He has worked in part-time football and so knows everything about it.”

This is clearly one of the challenges facing Arfield and all those like him who take a perceived backwards step into the part-time environment in order to go forward.

“That is something I spoke to part-time managers and ex-teammates about who have gone into management,” he says. “Boys have got jobs. This is a hobby. I don’t expect them to purely and utterly dedicate their lives like I have done for the last 20 years.”

But he does expect a degree of professionalism, something he will be bringing despite the inevitable frustrations of training just twice a week.

Four teams maintain close to full-time operations in League One, meaning East Fife must overachieve to prosper. Arfield is enthused by this challenge as well as by getting his boots back on. Agreeing to also play wasn't a stipulation for him getting the job. “There wasn’t any demand on it,” he says. “Any time I have been interviewed I have always said that I always want to continue playing. If you are playing, you are closer to the action.”

East Fife chairman Liam Anderson hailed the “massive appointment” for the club, and it’s true: not many teams at this level can claim to have a European finalist in the dugout.

Arfield came on as a sub for Rangers against Eintracht Frankfurt in the Europa League final in 2022, scoring a penalty in the shootout defeat. He aims to speak to Steven Gerrard, who signed him for Rangers, before too long but has already had plenty of support from Michael Beale, who followed Gerrard to Aston Villa before succeeding Giovanni van Bronckhorst at Ibrox.

“I certainly will be picking his (Gerrard’s) brains,” he says. “I loved Steven's calmness as a manager. I loved it. When he spoke, people listened.” Beale, who is currently fighting for his job at Belgian club Zulte Waregem, was one of Arfield’s first ports of call.

“‘Mick’, who perhaps didn’t have the reputation at the time, was very hands on,” he says. “Steven was more in the background, an authority figure.”

Beale was “delighted” when Arfield phoned to tell him the news. “He knows you cannot just go in at the top. Look at Steven Gerrard, one of the most iconic footballers there’s been.

“He has to go into an academy before coming up and winning a league in Scotland with the help of someone like Mick. He (Beale) knows it is a great situation. I think I am one of the first to get into management from that (Rangers) core group. He is delighted to see it happen.”

Whether Gerrard, who met up with Arfield at a recent Old Firm clash and slightly surreally commented “Yes Scotty lad” on an East Fife Instagram post announcing his appointment, takes in a game in Methil in the near future remains to be seen. He might be barred entry: World Cup winners only.

Erdem Is on a Red Carpet Style Streak—Here’s Why

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Celebrities from Kim Kardashian to Cynthia Erivo to Troye Sivan have pulled looks

At London Fashion Week this September, Erdem debuted a Spring 2027 collection that was instantly celebrated for its very on-brand tug-of-war between masculinity and femininity. Individual ensembles clashed crystal embellishment, tuxedo details, classic shirting, and unexpected flashes of skin. Critics praised it—and celebrity stylists began calling pieces in almost immediately.

Erdem has long had a slew of celebrity friends, supporters, and customers, but something about the reception this season feels different. In less than two months, runway looks have been worn by Kim Kardashian, Rosalía (twice), Cynthia Erivo, Sienna Miller, Troye Sivan, Honey Dijon, Cara Delevingne, Chappell Roan, Inde Navarette, Rosé, Keira Knightley, and Sarah Paulson. An unlikely collective perhaps, but each individual puts forth their own glorious interpretation of style and gender expression.

Erdem Moralıoğlu began this collection with the story of Barbette, the Texas-born trapeze artist whose performances, done in full drag, rose to prominence in Paris in the 1920s and ’30s. The clothes explore this sort of complex, androgynous dialogue between masculinity and femininity. The idea is perhaps best epitomized in a dress (worn by Knightley) that begins as a menswear-inspired gray knit before fading into a crystal-embellished sheer tulle skirt—and styled in the very Erdem manner with socks and black brogues. Or take Sivan’s ensemble: a black tuxedo jacket with satin and tulle draped over an ostrich feather pannier at the hip. Even the most markedly girlish looks, like Rosalía’s ’20s-inspired dress in blush pink silk, come with a certain restraint.

Moralıoğlu celebrated 20 years of Erdem this year and has built a ready-to-wear brand that is both distinctly recognizable and still able to evolve each season. Spring 2027 was not a radical departure for the designer, merely a looser approach to his typical decadence. (After all, Moralıoğlu has always paired his colorful jacquard dresses with simple black brogues.) It seems like a literal snapshot of consumers and their desires: clothes that combine elements of femininity and masculinity, utility and flourish. And it proves that two decades in, Moralıoğlu can continue to woo new audiences.

Ahead, we spoke with Moralıoğlu to gauge his reaction to all of this attention and reflect on what’s resonating most.

Well, the muse, as you know, was someone named “Barbette,” or Vander Clyde Broadway, who was originally from Austin, Texas, and then came to Paris and became this extraordinary muse and then kind of disappeared into obscurity. I love the idea of this idea of someone becoming something and that idea of self-actualization. But also, more importantly, Cocteau wrote a very interesting essay on Barbette and commissioned Man Ray to do this beautiful photo essay on him, as well. It was really about this idea of how relative masculinity and femininity is and about androgyny. So if I had to summarize it into one thing, there's a feeling of androgyny and flitting between masculine and feminine.

Yes, and I think what's interesting about Barbette as a character was that it was equally as feminine as it was masculine. In a way, they weren't trying to emulate becoming a woman, but they also weren't emulating a man. It's almost like the third sex, which is, I think, very interesting.

I definitely have noticed it, and it's definitely been noticed in the studio. All of us are like, "Wow, another one! It really seems to be happening.” But it's such a privilege to dress so many different people from so many different backgrounds and extraordinary, amazing, brilliant, talented people. I mean, it's wonderful. It's thrilling. I love it.

I mean, I can't pick just one. But I thought it was so interesting to see Troye in that piece, to see how that all worked and how it evolved. Interestingly enough, I had originally created trousers for that look, but didn’t use them for the show. So then the trousers came back out for that carpet. Otherwise, Troye was so loyal to how it was shown on the runway, even with the shirt styled exactly the same.

I still feel the same way. I think it's a privilege to be in a position where you dress extraordinarily talented people. That's a wonderful extension of your world that then becomes something else. It’s really interesting to see the collection take on another life. Otherwise, it just exists in the bubble of a show or an editorial.

The honest answer would be no, I don't think about it at all. I think about the collection and the character—her or them or whoever that person is. But when the show's coming together, you also really think about movement, how it's fitted, how something really moves on the body. And I wonder if that's something that's interesting in the context of it being outside of a runway show. I find Rosalia walking down the street in New York so fascinating. They're clothes that move and I really like that.

Well, I just left the other room where we’re working on the next one. But I like that psychology of turning the page. I find that really interesting. I love the idea of the blank, white page. I find it really exciting. But honest answer, no. [I don’t think about evolution]. Maybe what happens is that in time, you become a little bit looser. With that last show, there was something about that kind of world of Barbette. It was easy for us in the studio to really feel it, feel that masculine and feminine energy and to be very loose with it.

I usually tend to have a character. I always find it's like making a movie every six months. It's almost like a new script. I think it helps to take everyone collectively somewhere else. So you have to adjust together, but you have to have something to take everyone there with you.

Too difficult to say. In the context of these extraordinary women…Cynthia, I just think is amazing. Or Inde. And then Rosalía, Chappell, I'm like…

Let's think. Dietrich. [Marlene] Dietrich would be a good one. I love her.

Well, I think there’s something interesting about this idea of how you have a starting point and then you can end up somewhere else. How do you take the idea of something, and make it modern. How are you looking at something and finding its spirit? When you think about all of these questions of gender and expression, it's something that's always existed culturally. That's also a really beautiful thing.

United Adds Four Domestic Routes

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

United added four domestic routes to its 2027 schedule last week, a spokesperson confirmed to AirlineGeeks. Three of the new flights will depart from the carrier’s California hubs.

The airline will begin seasonal service from Los Angeles and San Francisco to Traverse City, Michigan, on June 19. Both routes will operate with Boeing 737-800s on Saturdays through Sept. 11, with additional flights on select Wednesdays. The additions will give Cherry Capital Airport nonstop service to all seven of United’s mainland hubs.

These additional routes were first flagged as part of last week’s Cirium Diio schedule update.

United will also fly year-round between San Francisco and Jacksonville, Florida, beginning March 29. The carrier will operate the route on Mondays and Fridays with a Boeing 737-800. Jacksonville Aviation Authority CEO Mark VanLoh said the Bay Area was “one of our largest unserved markets.”

San Francisco will become United’s sixth hub with nonstop service from Jacksonville.

The first of the four routes will start March 4, when United adds twice-daily flights between Lancaster, Pennsylvania, and Washington Dulles. The service restores a connection previously flown by Southern Airways Express. Southern’s Dulles flights ended in June when SkyWest began flying between Lancaster and Chicago O’Hare under the United Express banner as part of a federal Essential Air Service contract.

Why Scottish football must take John McGlynn's managerial talent more seriously as passionate case put forward

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Craig Levein believes Scottish football is guilty of overlooking one of its most impressive managerial talents, insisting John McGlynn has repeatedly demonstrated the qualities required to succeed at a bigger club – and even the Scotland national team.

The Falkirk manager continues to enhance his reputation after overseeing five consecutive victories, including Sunday’s 5-2 win over Hearts at Tynecastle, highlighting the consistency and quality of the work being carried out at the club. Yet, despite his dugout achievements at both Falkirk and Raith Rovers earning admiration, Levein is of the belief that McGlynn remains undervalued outside the clubs fortunate enough to have employed him.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Indeed, the former Scotland manager holds his old Tynecastle colleague in such high regard that he admits he would have handed him the national team reins. Having worked together in Gorgie, Levein insists McGlynn’s qualities were evident from the outset.

“I was promoting him for the Scotland job when it came around,” he confessed. “If you look at his record, it's pretty impressive. Raith Rovers and Falkirk, he’s pulling up trees at Falkirk just now. I just think he is undervalued, maybe not by the supporters that have had him as their manager but certainly in a wider sense in Scotland. He has done a brilliant job so far.

“When I went to Hearts at the tail end of my career, I was helping John do some training with the youngsters. Even then, you could tell immediately that he knows what he's talking about. John's eye for a player is pretty special. He's proved that over the years.”

It is that combination of football intelligence, vast experience, a calm temperament and attention to detail which makes Levein question why the meticulous 64-year-old boss has not attracted more interest from ambitious clubs operating at a higher level.

He suspects the changing trends in managerial appointments may play a part, with clubs influenced by perceptions surrounding age and reputation, rather than simply what a coach has achieved. Levein also believes there could be an element of snobbery when it comes to assessing managerial talent closer to home.

“If it was some foreign guy that had come in and done the same sort of job as him, I'm pretty sure he would have been promoted or moved to a higher level,” he stated. “I think he deserves to be working at a bigger club. I thought he had a chance to get the Scotland job. He's done all the work that he needs to do in my eyes to get an opportunity.”

There is little doubt that McGlynn's coaching methods have developed throughout his career. His time spent learning from Brendan Rodgers at Celtic allowed him to absorb new ideas and subsequently incorporate those principles into his own footballing philosophy.

Levein believes that demonstrated an ability to evolve without losing his own identity, explaining: “He went to Celtic, watched and learned everything on top of the knowledge he already had. The stuff he picked up from Brendan, he's implemented exceptionally well at Raith and at Falkirk. He's got integrity, knowledge, he delves into the minutiae when he's doing his team talks and makes sure everybody knows what's expected.”

Falkirk's recent performances have only strengthened the case. For Levein, there are no remaining questions about whether McGlynn can handle a greater challenge. The evidence is already there and becomes difficult to ignore.

“John's got better as he's grown older,” Levein added. “Quite a lot of times, guys who are coming in their 60s or 70s usually run out of energy. That's what catches up with guys getting into their later years. But he is relentless. Every box you want ticked, John would tick that box. I'm biased, I like the guy and what he does at football clubs. If I'm a chairman, all day long I'd be looking for somebody like him.

“He stays within the bandwidth of emotions. He doesn't get overly emotional during matches and he stays in this area, which I think is good for him and good for his players. You don't often see him throwing stuff about in the dugout or anything like that. He's a top manager who has improved enormously in the last five or six years.”

*Craig Levein was speaking at the announcement of William Hill as Official Sponsor and Proper Supporter of Walking Football Scotland. To find your nearest game visit walkingfootballscotland.org.

These M5 MacBook Air Laptops Are up to $250 Off Ahead of Black Friday

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Apple announced a slew of products in March, including the MacBook Air with the M5 chip, which ended up increasing in price in June due to the RAM shortage. These MacBooks come in both 13-inch and 15-inch sizes, and if you're interested in either, it's a good time to buy: In the leadup to Black Friday, Amazon is selling both at a $200 discount, and the 1 TB storage versions for $250 off.

Those discounts bring the 13-inch MacBook Air to $1,099.99 (down from $1,299) and $1,349.99 (originally $1,499.99) for the 512GB and 1 TB versions, respectively. The 15-inch MacBook Air is down to $1,299 (originally $1,499), and the 1TB version is $1,549.99 (originally $1,799.99). All versions are at their lowest prices since the June price hikes, according to tracking tools.

The M5 MacBook Air is the best laptop for most people looking for a new machine in 2026. In comparison to the already excellent M4 Airs, the M5 base model has double the storage at 512GB. The powerful M5 chip makes the performance even smoother according to Mashable's review. The aluminum design is as good as you'd expect from Apple's most popular portable laptop, lightweight and thin. It comes with a Liquid Retina display, Touch ID, a 12MP Center Stage Camera, and the Magic Keyboard.

Typically, the question would be whether to go for the slightly older M4 MacBook Air or pay a bit more for the newer M5 chip—but given how tough it is to find a new M4 laptop used, and the fact that most refurbished ones are the same price as the M5 with 512GB of storage, it makes the choice to go with the M5 a no-brainer—especially with a $250 discount.

32 Gifts For The Mom Who Has One Good Outfit & Wears It Everywhere

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

No wardrobe overhaul needed, just a few clever pieces to make her feel more put together.

Listen. Moms are juggling about a million things every minute of every day. We're bopping all over the place, half-frantic half the time, scooting kids from school to extracurriculars and keeping everyone's schedules from imploding and, and, and... the list is literally never-ending. So, yeah, maybe we reach for the same outfit or some spin on it every time we leave the house. Our unofficial uniform most days is black yoga pants and an oversized sweatshirt. And, sure, there are many days when we look like Adam Sandler's stunt double rolling through the school pickup line. Hey, whatever; it works. It's comfy and reliable.

That doesn't mean that even the most prolific outfit repeater hasn't caught a stray glimpse of herself in the mirror and thought, Hmm, my wardrobe could use a little love. Enter: these gifts. Trust, we're not here to talk anyone out of their uniform. (Respectfully, we would never.) We’re just talking about giving her a few pieces that will make her feel fresh and more put-together on the days she wants to try something different. A statement boot, a chic pullover, a bandana for a pop of print, jewelry she’ll never take off: small swaps that turn “I just threw this on” into “This is exactly the look I was going for” without a ton of effort or extra thought.

Diwali isn’t just a celebration that takes place throughout the South Asian diaspora. It’s an opportunity for South Asian creators to connect with local viewers by delivering the craft and game ideas that turn their celebrations into the talk of the town. Clearly, Priya’s videos are generating plenty of attention — that’s why hundreds of millions of viewers have decided to tune in.

Giada De Laurentiis Has 3 Tips For Buying The Highest Quality Pasta At The Grocery Store

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Every good pasta dish needs to be accompanied by a beautifully made sauce, but before the sauce, the cheese, and the garnish, there's the dried pasta itself. Of course, choosing the right shape counts, but that's only half the story. See, not all pasta brands are created equal. The next time you're walking down the pasta aisle, don't just pick one at random and hope for the best. Instead, borrow these three tips from Food Network legend Giada De Laurentiis to guarantee only the highest quality pasta ends up in your grocery cart. According to De Laurentiis, the pasta only needs water and durum wheat semolina, and it should be bronze-cut or slow-dried and have a rough texture.

De Laurentiis' tips are simple, as they are effective. When putting them all next to each other, the ingredients tip is arguably the most important by a significant margin. Semolina is a coarse, hard wheat flour with high protein and gluten levels, helping the pasta hold its shape and maintain its al dente bite. It should go without saying that dried pasta only needs water and durum wheat. No more, no less. In fact, for Italians, this doesn't represent high quality, but rather the baseline. Over there, the law mandates that any commercially sold "dried pasta" must be made strictly from 100% durum wheat semolina and water.

Unfortunately, the same rule doesn't apply here in the U.S., so some dried pastas are, for lack of better words, "enriched" with additives, preservatives, and certain brands, to the horror of Italians everywhere, even drop the semolina (which just refers to the coarse grind) in favor of finer-ground durum wheat flour, which just can't match the texture of semolina. If you spot any extra add-ins or swaps on the ingredient list, it's a telling sign that brand belongs nowhere near your plate.

We're putting Giada de Laurentiis' first tip ahead of the other two for a reason. If a box doesn't list just semolina durum wheat and water, chances are it won't pass these other checks either. Her second and third tips involve looking at the packaging. For high-quality pasta that goes above and beyond, look for "bronze cut" and "slow dried" on the box or bag.

Traditional artisan makers dry pasta slowly at low temperatures, and the most patient can take as long as six days. Mass-market brands don't have time to be sitting around, and so they blast the pasta with high heat and have it dry in a matter of hours — you can tell this was the case when the pasta is bright yellow rather than a pale, ivory white. This is about more than just cutting corners. Makers argue that low temperatures preserve the wheat's natural flavor and protect the gluten structure, which helps the pasta cook evenly and keep its bite.

Bronze cut refers to how the pasta is extruded (pushed through a die to be shaped), and it leads right into De Laurentiis' third tip. Traditional makers use dies made of bronze, which leave the pasta's surface rough, matte, and slightly powdery. Most mass-produced pasta is pushed through smooth, Teflon-coated dies instead, which leave it smooth and shiny. To some, this might be more aesthetically pleasing, but that stops mattering once the sauce enters the picture. Pasta's job is to carry the sauce, and the coarse surface of bronze-cut gives it more to cling to, while smooth pasta doesn't have a textured surface for the sauce to grip. Even if the label doesn't say bronze-cut, you can usually tell just by looking whether it has texture or not.

On this day in space! Nov. 9, 1967: NASA launches the Saturn V moon rocket for the first time on Apollo 4, but no astronauts were aboard

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

On Nov. 9, 1967, NASA launched the powerful new Saturn V rocket on its maiden voyage.

The Saturn V moon rocket was 363 feet tall. That's taller than the Statue of Liberty! It was the biggest and most powerful rocket ever built at the time (though SpaceX's Starship megarocket has since surpassed it). NASA needed such a powerful rocket to launch astronauts to the moon. However, the first Saturn V launch didn't carry astronauts, because it was only a test flight.

During the launch, the rocket created a loud shockwave that surprised nearby observers. The ground shook so hard that it rattled buildings miles away, and all the shaking caused a huge shower of dust and debris NASA's new launch center, the Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The mission lasted 8 hours 36 minutes. The rocket carried an uncrewed command module that splashed down in the Pacific Ocean.

The launch of Apollo 4 in November 1967 was a turning point for NASA on its path to landing astronauts on the moon.

The space agency had bet the moon on its new Saturn V booster, a three-stage heavy-lift rocket designed to fly astronauts in a capsule (as well as their lander) to the moon and back. But the rocket had never been fully tested until the Apollo 4 launch.

NASA also needed a new launch site for its Apollo missions. So the agency built Launch Complex 39 at what is now called the Kennedy Space Center, a Florida spaceport that has become the home port for orbital human spaceflight in the United States. But in 1967, no rockets had ever flown from the site. Yet, everything went according to plan.

“It was really an expert launching all the way through from lifting off exactly on time to performance of every single stage,” rocket pioneer Wernher von Braun, director of NASA’s Marshall Space Flight Center in Huntsville, Alabama, said at the time.

The launch came as NASA was on its heels.

Just months earlier, on Jan. 27, 1967, the space agency suffered a fatal tragedy when Apollo 1 astronauts Gus Grissom, Ed White and Roger Chaffee died on the launch pad after a fire broke out inside their Apollo command module during a ground test.

“The maiden voyage of the Saturn V dramatically increased the confidence of people across the nation in the management of the largest research and development undertaking in which the western world has ever engaged,” George Mueller, NASA's then-Associate Administrator for Manned Space Flight, said at the time.

One year later, another Saturn V rocket launched the first astronauts to the moon with the Apollo 8 mission. All of the Apollo missions that followed also launched on Saturn V rockets.

Just two years after its debut, the Saturn V rocket launched the Apollo 11 astronauts to the moon for the first crewed moon landing in July 1969.

Want more space history? Check out our full On This Day In Space Story archive and watch our On This Day In Space videos on YouTube.

CORRECTION: This story was updated at 7:26 pm ET to replace a launch photo to correctly show the Apollo 4 launch on a Saturn V rocket.

As an editor with over 10 years of experience in science journalism she has previously written for Scholastic Classroom Magazines, MedPage Today and The Joint Institute for Computational Sciences at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. After studying physics at the University of Tennessee in her hometown of Knoxville, she earned her graduate degree in Science, Health and Environmental Reporting (SHERP) from New York University. Hanneke joined the Space.com team in 2016 as a staff writer and producer, covering topics including spaceflight and astronomy. She currently lives in Seattle, home of the Space Needle, with her cat and two snakes. In her spare time, Hanneke enjoys exploring the Rocky Mountains, basking in nature and looking for dark skies to gaze at the cosmos.

The Supreme Court Just Gave Trump Approval to Build a Border Wall in Big Bend National Park

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Construction crews tear into Big Bend National Park to build the border wall (Photo: RONALDO SCHEMIDT / Getty Images)

Bulldozers may soon tear into the rock cliffs and desert sand in Big Bend National Park to erect parts of the Trump Administration’s controversial border barrier.

On November 9, the U.S. Supreme Court gave the federal government the green light to resume construction on barriers, fences, and surveillance equipment along the U.S.-Mexico border in a remote corner of Texas that includes Big Bend National Park.

In an unsigned opinion, the court ruled that Congress has expressed “clear preference for expeditious border construction.” The government’s desire to limit immigration overrules any “aesthetic, recreational, and environmental harm,” the court added.

The court ruled 8-1. Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson dissented with the decision.

The Center for Biological Diversity, one of the plaintiffs in a lawsuit that challenged the construction, blasted the decision in a statement.

“The Trump Administration and its contractors are hellbent on destroying one of America’s greatest national parks, and the Supreme Court just gave them the green light,” Laiken Jordahl, national public lands advocate at the Center for Biological Diversity, said on Facebook.

The ruling marks a groundbreaking decision in a months-long game of political football between the Trump Administration and a growing number of advocates, Texas locals, and lawmakers who opposed the construction.

Officials with the U.S. Customs and Border Protection and the Department of Homeland Security have said that the barriers along stretches of the Rio Grande and Big Bend National Park are needed to curb illegal immigrants from entering the U.S. from Mexico.

Advocates and local property owners in Texas have argued that the number of illegal crossings in the Big Bend region is negligible, and that construction would irreparably damage the pristine landscape, hurt local flora and fauna, and destroy local businesses that rely on the landscape for tourism and agriculture. In April, a coalition of opponents to the project filed suit to block the construction.

In August, construction crews arrived in the Big Bend region and began clearing swaths of desert for bollards and fencing. But construction ground to a halt after state politicians put pressure on Trump administration officials. And on October 2, a federal judge overseeing the legal case ruled that the construction should stop because it threatened to harm a group of plaintiffs who had sued to stop it.

The border wall plan drew ire from officials on both sides of the political spectrum. On November 9, Texas Senator John Cornyn, a Republican, published an op-ed in the San Antonio Express-News titled “The Case Against the Big Bend Border Wall.”

“Texans should not have to bear another unnecessary financial burden caused by building a 30-foot wall atop a 1,000-foot riverbank cliff,” Cornyn wrote.

After the Supreme Court decision on November 9, environmental groups and other opponents voiced their disapproval on social media.

“We are outraged by the Supreme Court’s decision allowing Trump’s border wall construction to resume in Big Bend National Park,” the Sierra Club posted on X. “This destructive project threatens irreplaceable wildlife habitat, sacred cultural sites, and one of America’s most treasured landscapes.”

In a statement, Cary Dupuy, Texas Regional Director for the National Parks Conservation Association (NPCA), said the decision would have “profound consequences.”

“Our national parks are meant to be set aside from harmful development and safeguarded under the highest standard possible under American law,” Dupy said. “Regardless of the court’s decision, we urge the administration to implement alternatives to damaging border barriers and other heavy infrastructure in this wild place. There is still a chance for us to save Big Bend, and there is still an opportunity for the administration to change course.”

Meghan Riley, senior attorney with the environmental advocacy group Earthjustice, called Big Bend a “national treasure.”

“allowing the administration to bulldoze through these fragile and protected ecosystems puts irreplaceable land at risk,” Riley said in a statement. “Once this damage is done, there is no simply putting Big Bend back together.”

Dave Cortez, director of the Sierra Club’s Lone Star chapter, pointed out the bipartisan anger at the border construction within Texas.

“Millions of Texans turned out to vote for change last week, and Republicans, Democrats, and Independents alike have spoken out against the Trump administration’s reckless and destructive plans to bulldoze the treasured borderlands of Far West Texas and the greater Big Bend region,” Cortez said in a statement. “This Supreme Court decision ignores the bipartisan opposition to a project that threatens some of Texas’s most cherished landscapes.”

Flight Attendant Finds Hidden Camera In Lavatory—Passenger Charged With Trying To Erase Evidence

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A flight attendant found a hidden camera in a Scoot lavatory. The passenger onboard the Singapore Airlines low cost carrier accused of putting it there is also charged with deleting evidence. It’s the deleting that creates the greatest risk of imprisonment. The incident happened October 7 on Scoot flight TR821 from Osaka to Singapore. The 52-year-old British passenger was charged in Singapore on November 5. One Mile at a Time flags the story noting he’s currently jailed. He’s now been offered S$10,000 bail. The man plans to plead guilty. According to the Singapore Police Force, the man concealed a camera in a lavatory, left, and activated recording through an app on his phone. A female flight attendant discovered the device while using the lavatory. She removed it and alerted a colleague. Police say Lutley later tried to retrieve it, found it missing, and deleted the app and related photographs to avoid detection. Officers detained him when the flight arrived at Changi Airport and seized both his phone and the camera. That’s some fancy police work! The Alleged Deletion Carries The Biggest Penalty He faces one count each of: Voyeurism: up to two years in prison, with a fine also available. Obstructing the course of justice: up to seven years in prison, a fine, or both, over the alleged deletion of the app and photographs. While the voyeurism law includes caning, Singapore exempts men over 50 so he’s in the clear there. They also recovered deleted files showing women in an Emirates lavatory, including a flight attendant. Avedon Director Ron Howard On Richard Avedon’s “Creative Courage,” and His Favorite Images From 'In the American West'

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In his new documentary Avedon, director Ron Howard traces the vivid legacy of the influential photographer Richard Avedon, which premieres November 11 on Netflix. “The subjects he worked with on the commercial side of his career were dazzling and beautiful, and he was so entertaining and expressive in the way that he would shoot them. But I really had no idea how he applied his status, his influence, his access to tell this other kind of story about human beings that was honest, insightful, and more journalistic,” Howard tells Vanity Fair.

The glamorous heights of Avedon’s career were what first caught Howard’s attention. “I became aware of him really in the ’80s. Then it was even his TV commercials, like Brooke Shields [in Calvin Klein ads],” Howard said. Later, he realized a few of his favorite portraits were taken by Avedon. One of these was a portrait of a young Kareem Abdul-Jabbar at a playground in New York holding a basketball “and kind of casually standing there looking straight into the camera. It’s not against a white background…” notes Howard. “I know Kareem a little bit. It so captured the foundational strength of character and intelligence that Kareem carried with him along with his basketball talent.”

Initially, Howard, who spent three years making the film, thought it was going to be more focused on the fashion and celebrity worlds that Avedon inhabited. However, his fascination with photographs like “sad Marilyn” (Avedon’s 1957 portrait of Marilyn Monroe) grew into a respect for the full breadth of Avedon’s career, and his “creative courage as an artist.”

In the film, footage of Avedon and new interviews with Lauren Hutton, Tina Brown, Adam Gopnik, Hilton Als, Joel Meyerowitz, Larry Gagosian, and Avedon’s son John, among others, paint a picture of a driven, curious, and sometimes mercurial artist. In the 1950s, Avedon’s career took off when he began shooting for Harper’s Bazaar, Vogue, and Vanity Fair. He injected a sense of play and prewar European glamour into his images by drawing inspiration from the films of Fred Astaire and Ginger Rogers. Eventually, he ventured into documentary assignments and more political subjects, including Nothing Personal (1964), a photobook with text by James Baldwin that captured the tensions of the Civil Rights era alongside a broader look at American life, a three panel portrait of anti-war protesters the Chicago Seven, and members of America’s political and financial elite at the time of the country’s bicentennial (“The Family”) for Rolling Stone. At the height of his career, he seemingly shifted quite easily from photographing celebrities to documenting anti-war protests and the counterculture of the 1960s, even when his work was met with mixed reactions.

Dovima with elephants, evening dress by Dior, Cirque d’Hiver, Paris, August 1955.

James Baldwin, New York, January 9, 1963.

Avedon (left) with Gloria Vanderbilt and Sidney Lumet at a party for the movie premiere of East of Eden (dir. Elia Kazan), 1955.

“We tried to reflect the risks he took and the transitions he made with his art and his career,” Howard shares. Had Howard made the documentary in Avedon’s lifetime, he would have liked to have asked him directly about the turning points in his career. “In all the interviews, he just kind of made it seem like it was a natural step. But to me, it's not a natural step to stop getting thousands and thousands of dollars an hour to shoot commercials and shoot for the world's most read magazines, and go to Vietnam where no one was really. He wasn’t on assignment initially… he just committed.”

In 1979, the Amon Carter Museum of American Art in Fort Worth, Texas, commissioned Avedon to capture a portrait of the West. He worked on this ambitious portfolio periodically through 1984, traveling across 17 states like California, Colorado, Iowa, North Dakota, Texas, and Wyoming, with assistants and a researcher, Laura Wilson, who helped Avedon arrange 752 portrait sittings. (Wilson also documented the making of the project in her own remarkable photographs and writing.) In total, Avedon printed 124 photographs (some as large as four feet tall) and exhibited them at the museum in 1985. The result was In the American West—a turning point in Avedon’s career. Prior to the project’s completion, in 1983, Vanity Fair ran five photographs from the eventual series, showing coal miners covered in dust and one lone oil worker alongside a feature on Wagner’s operas and an excerpt of Gabriel García Márquez’s Chronicle of a Death Foretold.

“One of the reasons I was drawn to [In the American West] is because my family roots are in the Midwest,” Howard says. “I see a lot of truth in these images.”

Avedon conducting a portrait session for In the American West beside his 8x10 camera.

In a similar fashion to August Sander’s massive undertaking People of the 20th Century, Avedon went looking for “men and women who work at hard, uncelebrated jobs, the people who are often ignored or overlooked,” Wilson wrote. In doing so he joined a whole legion of photographers whose images have come to define broad swaths of middle and western America. But unlike common readings of Sander’s project at the time, Avedon was clear that his version of the West was totally subjective, more of a cinematic panorama than an objective record. He was emphatic that all photographs are filtered through the photographer’s eye, and become their interpretation. Through Wilson, he declared, “This is a fictional West. I don’t think the West of these portraits is any more conclusive than the West of John Wayne.”

Below, Howard reflects on five of his favorite photographs from Avedon’s In the American West.

“I asked if that was makeup, and I was assured that none of it was. [Avedon] set up, and just as workers were coming off their shift, he was getting them in front of the camera. I mean, wow, this is what these workers look like. This is what was in their lungs and their pores. I have cousins and uncles who worked in the oil fields. I never had any idea that they had their days looking like they’d been through what this guy has been through on his shift.”

“The boy with the rattlesnake was the first photo that [Avedon] took where he knew he was going to get at something deeper... He felt like this was almost religious iconography in a way, or mythic. [The subject] was a teenager. As Avedon says, he's a boy turning into a man, and here he's doing this kind of work, so it speaks to a transformation. It also speaks of that region as a very pragmatic region. My own dad, to try to get some spending money [in the ’30s], was hunting and trapping raccoons to sell skins to the junk dealer or possums they could eat. Absolutely just a pragmatic way of life there. So that image always resonated with me. It is also featured in the documentary because it was the proof in [Avedon’s] mind that he was going to be able to identify moments and individuals that would be arresting and mean a lot on an artistic level.”

“The beekeeper is, of course, a stunning image–one that doesn't have that much to do with the American West. I think [Avedon] could have found somebody like that in almost any corner of the planet, but it certainly is fascinating and a tribute to the research team that was working with him to help find him.”

“The maintenance worker who’s dressed up in a really flashy suit, suit jacket, tie and hat, that just looks very familiar to me. I mean, the jacket and all the embroidery on it, the flowers, I never saw anything like that with my family. They didn’t have that kind of flourish. This guy looks like he could be a country western singer or could be trying to be a country western star. But the hat, the tie, the gaze, the posture looks very, very familiar to me. That looks like a hardworking guy getting dressed up on the weekend.”

Jupiter and Mars are set for a breathtaking close approach later this week: Here's how to see it

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Jupiter and Mars will appear incredibly close as they shine in the constellation Leo on Nov. 15.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Jupiter and Mars will shine side by side in a spectacular close approach in the heart of the constellation Leo on Nov. 15. Here's what you need to know.

Mars and Jupiter reach their closest apparent separation in Earth's sky at 9:04 p.m. EST on Nov. 15 (0204 GMT on Nov. 16), according to astronomy website In-The-Sky. The planets will be below the horizon for U.S. stargazers at the moment of their closest approach, but observers can catch the pair rising above the eastern horizon around midnight local time, just a few hours later.

Remember: You can discover the exact timings for the rising and setting of the planets above your local horizon using Time and Date's night sky tracker.

Jupiter will be the brightest of the pair, when it rises a little over 1 degree from the red light of Mars — roughly the width of your little finger held at arm's length against the night sky. The bright star Regulus will glow close at hand, forming the dot of the backward question mark formation of stars that represents the head of the great Lion in the constellation Leo.

The Celestron StarSense Explorer LT114AZ is a great way to get a closer look at Jupiter, Mars and Saturn. Its 4.5-inch aperture and a high 1000mm focal length delivers good magnification out of the box, plus it's integrated with the StarSense smartphone app, which makes finding those planets so much easier. Check out our full review for more.

Mars and Jupiter will be close enough to share the field of view of a pair of 10x50 binoculars and will also make for a spectacular sight through the eyepiece of a telescope when viewed separately as they rise away from the horizon.

The gas giant's iconic Great Red Spot will rotate into view in the predawn hours of Nov. 16, and will be visible through a backyard telescope with an aperture of just 4 inches. Using a light green color filter will help increase the visibility of the vast storm, while an orange filter will help bring out detail in the gas giant's sweeping cloud belts.

The two planets will appear to gradually drift apart over the coming weeks and won't appear as close again for another 2.5 years in July 2029, when the two planets appear close together in the constellation Virgo.

Be sure to check out our guide to photographing the night sky, along with our picks of the best cameras and lenses for astrophotography if you're interested in following the dance of the planets for yourself.

DCU shake-up: James Gunn and Peter Safran out post-merger

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Producer and former Kevin Feige protege Jeremy Latcham will replace Gunn and Safran.

It’s barely been two months since the controversial Paramount and Warner Bros. merger, and we’ve already got a shockingly unexpected ouster: Co-CEOs of the rebooted DC Universe, James Gunn and Peter Safran, are stepping down from that role despite the Hollywood rumor mill assuming that the duo would be safe.

It’s unclear whether the pair chose to resign or were forced out, or what will happen to their planned slate of projects beyond 2028’s Superman: Man of Tomorrow, which is in post-production. Producer Jeremy Latcham—a protege of Marvel’s Kevin Feige who worked with Gunn on Guardians of the Galaxy among other MCU films—will replace them. Per Gunn and Safran’s joint statement:

Four years ago, we set out to unite DC under one creative vision. In that time, we helped restore Superman to the heart of DC, while opening new doors and telling new stories in film and television with Peacemaker, Creature Commandos, Lanterns and Clayface. We’re immensely proud of the team that turned that ambition into something real, including the actors, crews and executives we had the opportunity to collaborate with. We’ve shared a creative history with Jeremy going back to Marvel, and we’re eager to see the perspective he brings to DC’s next chapter. We’re excited to return to what drew us to this job in the first place, which is telling the stories we love, including the next chapter of Clark and Lex’s journey in Man of Tomorrow.

DCU fans are mourning the news and what it could mean for the franchise. My feelings are mixed. On the one hand, I’m on record that I’ve found the rebooted DC Universe films released thus far to be enjoyable but rather forgettable, even when the positives outweigh the negatives. (I have yet to see Clayface, which debuted to good reviews and a solid box office showing.) But HBO has done right by the DCU: I loved both seasons of Peacemaker; Lanterns is a shining beacon of what is possible when someone is allowed to get really creative within the genre; and I’m very much looking forward to next year’s Jimmy Olsen (Skylar Gisondo) series The People v. Gorilla Grodd—described as a “mockumentary crime comedy superhero series.”

I also love Gunn’s more irreverent sensibility with superhero fare and his rapport with fans. At least now he will get to focus on what he does best—and Gunn seems to feel the same way, per his personal Instagram post. Gunn says he had mixed feelings about taking the job at first but “couldn’t pass up the unique opportunity,” adding that he has no regrets and is glad he took it.

But Gunn also noted that he sometimes found the job trying, with very little time to recharge between projects; that his ADHD made it challenging to juggle so many projects at once; and that he disliked “the at-times corporate nature of the job,” thanking Safran for “taking the brunt of that.” He concluded, “I will miss being the co-caretaker of these great characters, but I’m also incredibly relieved and excited to get back to pure filmmaking.”

While Gunn and Safran reported directly to Warner Bros. Discovery CEO David Zaslav—unusual for the industry—Latcham will report to Dana Goldberg and Josh Greenstein, co-chairs of the Skydance Motion Picture Group. “Jeremy brings a deep reverence for DC and its passionate fanbase, recognizing that they are the very foundation of this storied, multigenerational brand,” Goldberg and Greenstein said in a statement. “Under his leadership, every film and television show will be ushered from start to finish through the prism of the legions of fans who love and champion it. Jeremy’s track record exemplifies the excellence he will bring to building out DC and the many beloved characters within it.”

DC’s pantheon of characters is among the most iconic in American popular culture. I’ve had the privilege to help bring some of the most celebrated superhero stories to life on the big screen, and I’m excited and honored for the opportunity to now lead DC Studios into its next era. The chance to steward the incredible cast of DC characters to the screen in a way that excites lifelong fans while introducing their timeless stories to the next generation is a massive responsibility that I do not take lightly. I am grateful to Dana and Josh for putting their trust in me. I can’t wait to work night and day alongside filmmakers, comic book creators, artists and storytellers of every kind, across film, television and animation, to build something ambitious for longtime DC fans and newcomers alike.

Latcham has the superhero bona fides, having been a producer on some of Marvel’s biggest hits: Guardians, Iron Man, Iron Man 2, The Avengers, Age of Ultron, and 2017’s Spider-Man: Homecoming. He left to create his own production company, which spearheaded 2023’s Dungeons and Dragons: Honor Among Thieves and 2018’s Bad Times at the El Royale.

The DCU should be in capable hands, even if the overall vision changes. I’d like to see the DCU move away from the connected universe model and back to standalone films where the storytelling and character development aren’t beholden to setting up the next three films. But I’m not the head of a major studio.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.
